**Driver requirement**

> ● 32-bit: 32-bit drivers only
>
> ● 64-bit: Requires 64-bit drivers

**Current relevance**

> ● 32-bit: Largely obsolete
>
> ● 64-bit: Standard for all modern systems

### **Installation Methods**

**In-place Upgrade** — Installs new OS over existing one; keeps files,
settings, apps

**Clean Install** — Wipes drive and installs fresh; no legacy
data/settings carried over

**Unattended Install** — Uses an answer file to automate installation
without user input

**Image Deployment** — Deploys a pre-configured OS image to multiple
machines (common in enterprise)

**Remote Network Install** — Installs OS over the network from a shared
installation source (e.g., PXE boot)

**Repair Install** — Reinstalls OS files while preserving user data/apps
— fixes corruption

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** A 64-bit OS cannot run on hardware that only supports
32-bit drivers, and a 32-bit OS can't utilize more than ~4 GB of RAM — a
classic exam trap when a machine “has 8GB RAM installed but only shows
3.5GB usable.”

## Windows System Tools

**Task Manager**\
Purpose: View CPU/RAM usage, stop unresponsive apps, manage startup.\
Common Uses: Slow performance, malware-like behavior.

**Event Viewer**\
Purpose: Displays system logs to diagnose issues.\
Common Uses: Boot issues, application crashes, driver errors.

**Device Manager**\
Purpose: Manages hardware and device drivers.\
Common Uses: Enable/disable devices, roll back or update drivers.

**Disk Management**\
Purpose: Tool for managing storage devices and partitions.\
Common Uses: Creating partitions, formatting disks, assigning drive
letters.

## Command-Line Tools

**cd** — Changes the current directory.

**dir** — Lists files and folders in the current directory.

**md (or mkdir)** — Creates a new directory.

**rd (or rmdir)** — Removes a directory.

**ipconfig** — Shows IP configuration; use /release + /renew for network
issues.

**ping** — Tests network connectivity.

**tracert** — Traces the network path (hops) to a destination.

**nslookup** — Queries DNS to resolve a domain name to an IP address.

**netstat** — Shows active network connections.

**sfc /scannow** — Repairs Windows system files.

**DISM** — Repairs the Windows system image; often used alongside sfc
/scannow when SFC alone can't fix corruption.

**chkdsk** — Checks disk integrity and fixes file system issues.

**bootrec** — Repairs bootloader problems (MBR/BCD).

**gpupdate /force** — Forces Group Policy to reapply immediately.

**gpresult /r** — Shows applied Group Policies on the PC.

**tasklist** — Lists running processes and their Process ID (PID).

**taskkill /PID \<PID\>** — Terminates processes. Must include PID Code

**diskpart** — Advanced partition manager for disks and volumes.

**format** — Formats a drive with a file system such as NTFS or FAT32.

**robocopy** — Copy tool supporting resume, multi-threading, and backup
modes.

**net use** — Maps or disconnects network drives and shared folders.

**shutdown** — Shuts down or restarts the system (/s shutdown, /r
restart, /t time).

**pathping** — Combines ping and tracert functionality. Shows packet
loss statistics at each hop along a route, useful for identifying which
specific hop is causing degraded performance.

**net user** — Displays or modifies local user account information from
the command line.

**winver** — Opens a dialog showing the exact Windows version and build
number currently installed.

**whoami** — Displays the currently logged-in username. Useful for
confirming which account context a command prompt or script is running
under.

## Users, Groups, and Permissions

Windows uses groups to simplify management. Instead of assigning
permissions to each user one by one, technicians assign rights to the
group.

> ● **Administrators** — Members have full control over the system.
>
> ● **Users (Standard Users)** — Members have limited permissions and
> cannot make system-wide changes.
>
> ● **Guests** — Highly restricted access, typically disabled for
> security.
>
> ● **Remote Desktop Users** — Allowed to sign in via Remote Desktop.
>
> ● **Backup Operators** — Can backup and restore files regardless of
> permissions.

**NTFS Permissions:** Determine which users or groups can read, modify,
delete, or execute files and folders on an NTFS-formatted drive.
Supports inheritance - permissions flow from parent folders to
subfolders.

**Sharing Permissions:** Control how users access a folder over the
network via SMB. When NTFS + Share permissions conflict → the most
restrictive wins.

## Windows Networking Configuration

**Workgroup** — Peer-to-peer network model; each PC manages its own
local accounts and permissions. Best for small setups (under ~10 PCs).

**Domain** — Centralized network model where a server (domain
controller) manages accounts, security policies, and authentication for
all connected PCs.

**Homegroup (deprecated)** — Legacy Windows feature for simple home
file/printer sharing; removed starting Windows 10 v1803.

**Mapped Network Drive** — Assigns a drive letter to a shared folder on
another PC/server for easy access.

**Shared Resources** — Folders, printers, or drives made accessible to
other users on the network via file/printer sharing settings.

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** The exam tests this in scenario form. If the question
mentions a dedicated server, domain, or Active Directory — that's
client-server. If it mentions a small office with no server where users
share files directly — that's peer-to-peer.

## Essential Linux Commands

**ls** — List files in a directory.

**pwd** — Show current directory.

**cd** — Change directories.

**mkdir** — Create a new directory.

**mv** — Move or rename files and directories.

**cp** — Copy files or directories.

**rm** — Remove (delete) files.

**cat** — Display the contents of a file.

**grep** — Search for text within files.

**chmod** — Change file/folder permissions.

**chown** — Change file/folder ownership.

**su** — Switch to another user account (e.g., root); stays elevated
until exit.

**sudo** — Run a single command with administrative privileges.

**passwd** — Change a user account's password.

**ps / top** — View running processes.

**df** — Show disk space usage and availability.

**man** — Display the manual/help page for a command.

**shutdown** — Shut down or restart the system (e.g., shutdown -r to
reboot).

**ifconfig / ip** — View or configure network settings.

**apt-get / dpkg** — Package management for Debian/Ubuntu-based systems.

**yum / dnf** — Package management for Red Hat/Fedora-based systems.

**find** — Searches for files/directories by name, type, or other
criteria.

**fsck** — Checks and repairs filesystem errors (Linux equivalent of
chkdsk).

**mount** — Attaches a filesystem/drive to a specific directory so it
can be accessed.

**ip** — Modern command for viewing/configuring network interfaces
(replacing ifconfig).

**ping** — Tests network connectivity to a host.

**curl** — Transfers data to/from a server using various protocols
(HTTP, FTP, etc.).

**dig** — Queries DNS servers for domain information (Linux equivalent
of nslookup).

**traceroute** — Traces the network path to a destination (Linux
equivalent of Windows' tracert).

**systemd** — The modern Linux init system and service manager
responsible for booting the system, starting services, and managing
system state on most current Linux distributions.

**/etc/resolv.conf** — Configuration file specifying which DNS servers
the system uses for name resolution.

**/etc/fstab** — Configuration file listing storage devices and
partitions to be automatically mounted at boot, along with their mount
points and options.

## macOS Essentials

**Finder** — The macOS file manager used to navigate files, manage
storage, and organize applications.

**System Settings** — Central location for configuring network, users,
displays, and privacy settings.

**Terminal** — Command-line interface for advanced tasks.

**Disk Utility** — Used for formatting drives, partitioning, and running
First Aid to repair file system issues.

**Activity Monitor** — Shows CPU, memory, energy, disk, and network
usage (macOS version of Task Manager).

**Time Machine** — Built-in backup solution that creates continuous
versioned snapshots for easy system restore.

**Force Quit** — Terminates an unresponsive application (macOS
equivalent of Windows Task Manager's “End Task”).

**Spotlight** — System-wide search utility (Cmd+Space) for finding
files, apps, and content, including web results.

**Keychain** — Built-in password management system that stores and
auto-fills saved credentials.

**FileVault** — Full-disk encryption feature for macOS, similar in
purpose to Windows BitLocker.

**iCloud** — Apple's cloud storage/sync service, integrating Drive,
iMessage, FaceTime, and cross-device continuity.

**Gestures** — Multi-touch trackpad/touchscreen input for navigation
(swipes, pinches, multi-finger taps).

**Dock** — The macOS application launcher/taskbar at the bottom of the
screen for launching and switching apps.

**Continuity** — Feature allowing seamless handoff of tasks (calls,
messages, copy/paste) between Apple devices.

**Mission Control** — macOS feature providing an overview of all open
windows, desktops (Spaces), and full-screen apps, accessed via a swipe
gesture or dedicated key, for quick navigation between running tasks.

**Rapid Security Response (RSR**) — Apple's mechanism for delivering
critical security patches to macOS and iOS outside of the normal full OS
update cycle, allowing urgent fixes to be applied faster without a full
system update.

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** FileVault (macOS) and BitLocker (Windows) are commonly
paired in comparison questions — both provide full-disk encryption, but
are platform-specific. Similarly, Force Quit (macOS) and End Task
(Windows Task Manager) serve the same purpose across platforms.

## Quick Review — Windows OS & Software

### Questions

1.  A Windows 10 Home user needs to join their company's Active
    Directory domain. What is the minimum edition upgrade required?

2.  A technician runs \`sfc /scannow\` but it reports it cannot fix
    corrupted files. What command should be run first before retrying
    sfc?

3.  What PowerShell execution policy allows local scripts to run but
    requires downloaded scripts to be digitally signed?

4.  What is the correct command to force Group Policy to reapply
    immediately on a Windows workstation?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  Windows 10 Pro — Domain join, BitLocker, Group Policy, Hyper-V, and
    Remote Desktop Host all require Pro minimum (not available in Home
    edition).

2.  DISM /Online /Cleanup-Image /RestoreHealth— DISM repairs the Windows
    component store that sfc relies on. Always run DISM before retrying
    sfc if the first scan fails.

3.  RemoteSigned— Local scripts run freely; downloaded scripts must be
    digitally signed by a trusted publisher. This is the recommended
    policy for most environments.

4.  gpupdate /force — Forces Group Policy to reapply immediately without
    waiting for the automatic refresh cycle.

# Module: Security

## Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Identify and mitigate common security threats (malware, unsafe
> networks, unauthorized access).
>
> ● Understand workstation, mobile, and network security best practices.
>
> ● Apply authentication, permission, and encryption controls.
>
> ● Remove malware using CompTIA’s recommended process.
>
> ● Recognize and prevent social engineering attacks.
>
> ● Configure wireless security and physical device protection.

## Common Types of Malware

**Virus** — Attaches to legitimate files and spreads when executed.

**Worm** — Self-replicates across networks without user action.

**Trojan** — Appears legitimate but contains harmful code.

**Spyware** — Steals sensitive information such as passwords or browsing
data.

**Ransomware** — Encrypts user data and demands payment for decryption.

**Rootkit** — Hides deep within the OS to conceal attackers or malware.

**Adware** — Displays unwanted ads, sometimes malicious.

**Fileless Malware** — Resides in memory using legitimate system tools
instead of files.

**Keyloggers** — Covertly records user keystrokes to steal data.

**Stalkerware** — Covert monitoring software installed on a device
(often by someone with prior physical access) to secretly track a
person's location, messages, calls, or activity.

**Cryptominer (Cryptojacking)** — Malware that hijacks a device's
CPU/GPU resources to mine cryptocurrency for the attacker without the
owner's consent, causing high resource usage and system slowdown.

**Boot Sector Virus** — Malware that infects the master boot record
(MBR) or boot sector of a storage device, executing before the operating
system loads, making it difficult to detect and remove with standard
OS-level antivirus tools.

## Threats

**SQL Injection** — An attack where malicious SQL code is inserted into
an input field (like a login form) to manipulate or extract data from a
backend database.

**Business Email Compromise (BEC)** — A targeted social engineering
attack where an attacker impersonates an executive or trusted vendor via
email to trick an employee into making a fraudulent wire transfer or
divulging sensitive data. Distinct from generic phishing due to its
highly targeted, research-driven nature.

## 7-Step Malware Removal Procedure

Memorizing the exact chronological order of this process is
non-negotiable for the Core 2 exam. If a system is compromised, you must
execute these steps sequentially to prevent the spread of the infection
and ensure total remediation.

**Step 1** — Identify and research malware symptoms: Do not assume;
verify. Ask the user what they were doing when the issue started. Look
for redirected browsers, fake antivirus alerts, or unusually high
CPU/network usage.

**Step 2** — Quarantine the infected system: Disconnect the network
cable and disable Wi-Fi immediately! Your primary goal is to prevent the
malware from spreading across the corporate network. Do not power off
the machine, as you may lose valuable volatile memory (RAM) data.

**Step 3** — Disable System Restore (Windows): Malware often hides
copies of itself inside Windows System Restore points. If you do not
disable this feature, the malware will simply reinstall itself the next
time the system reboots.

**Step 4** — Remediate the infected system: This is a two-part phase:\
1. Update: Manually update your anti-malware signatures.\
2. Scan: Boot the computer into Safe Mode or a pre-installation
environment (WinPE) and run the removal tool.\
3. Reimage/Reinstall (if needed): If removal tools can't fully clean the
system or the infection is severe (rootkit-level), the safest
remediation is wiping the drive and reimaging/reinstalling the OS from a
known-clean source.

**Step 5** — Schedule updates and additional scans: Once the immediate
threat is removed, automate your defenses. Configure the anti-malware
software to pull daily signature updates and run full system scans
automatically.

**Step 6** — Enable System Restore and create a restore point: Now that
the system is 100% clean, turn System Restore back on. Manually create a
brand-new, malware-free restore point so the user has a safe fallback
state.

**Step 7** — Educate the user: The final step is always training. Teach
the user how to identify phishing emails, avoid suspicious downloads,
and recognize social engineering tactics to prevent a repeat incident.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** The “Skip a Step” Scenario - CompTIA frequently uses
> multiple-choice questions designed to trick you into performing
> actions out of order.

## Detection Response tool

**EDR (Endpoint Detection and Response)** — Security software that
continuously monitors endpoint devices for suspicious activity,
providing detection, investigation, and automated response capabilities
beyond traditional antivirus.

**MDR (Managed Detection and Response)** — A service where a third-party
security provider operates EDR-like monitoring and response on an
organization's behalf, typically including 24/7 human analyst oversight.

**XDR (Extended Detection and Response)** — Extends EDR's
endpoint-focused monitoring across multiple security layers (endpoints,
network, email, cloud) into a single correlated detection and response
platform.

**Recovery Console** — A Windows repair environment/tool (accessed via
WinRE) providing command-line access to repair a system that won't boot
normally.

## Wi-Fi Security Protocols

**WEP**\
Description: Very old; easily cracked; should never be used.\
Security Level:Very Weak

**WPA**\
Description: Early improvement over WEP, but outdated.\
Security Level:Weak

**WPA2**\
Description: Modern baseline standard; commonly used.\
Security Level:Strong

**WPA3**\
Description: Latest and most secure standard.\
Security Level:Very Strong

## Social Engineering Attack Types

**Phishing / Spear-Phishing / Whaling / Quishing** — Fraudulent messages
designed to trick users into revealing sensitive data. Spear-phishing
targets specific individuals; whaling targets executives; quishing uses
QR codes.

**Vishing** — Voice-based phishing using phone calls to extract
information.

**Smishing** — SMS/text-based phishing attempts.

**Impersonation** — Attacker pretends to be someone trusted (IT staff,
coworker, vendor).

**Tailgating / Piggybacking** — Gaining physical access by following
someone into a secured area.

**Pretexting** — Attackers create a fabricated scenario to obtain
information or access.

## Encryption & Data Protection

> ● Full-Disk Encryption: BitLocker (Windows) and FileVault (macOS)
> encrypt entire drives to protect data if a device is lost or stolen.
>
> ● VPN for public Wi-Fi: Creates a secure, encrypted tunnel for data in
> transit.
>
> ● HTTPS / TLS: Ensures encrypted web browsing.

## Physical Security Controls

### Deterrent Controls

**Security Signage** — Warns unauthorized users to stay out.

**Visible Cameras** — Reduces attempted theft or intrusion.

**Exterior Lighting** — Removes hiding spots and improves visibility.

### Preventive Controls

**Badge / Keycard Access** — Restricts entry to authorized employees.

**Biometric Locks** — Fingerprint or facial authentication.

**Mantraps** — Two-door system preventing tailgating.

**Bollards** — Short, sturdy vertical posts installed around a
building's perimeter or entrance to block vehicle access while still
allowing pedestrian access. A physical deterrent/prevention control.

**Magnetometers** — Metal-detection devices used at secure entry points
to screen individuals for concealed metallic weapons or unauthorized
devices before granting building access.

**Cable Locks** — Secure laptops, desktops, and projectors.

### Detective Controls

**CCTV Monitoring** — Records activity for investigation.

**Motion Sensors** — Detects unauthorized movement.

**Door Access Logs** — Tracks who entered and when.

## Windows Security Features

### Key Windows Security Features

**BitLocker** — Full-disk encryption built into Windows Pro/Enterprise

**Windows Defender Firewall** — Built-in host-based firewall controlling
inbound/outbound traffic

**UAC (User Account Control)** — Prompts for permission before allowing
changes requiring admin rights

**Windows Defender Antivirus** — Built-in real-time malware protection

**BitLocker To Go** — Extends BitLocker's full-disk encryption to
removable USB drives, protecting data on portable media if lost or
stolen.

**Power User** — A legacy Windows account type with more privileges than
a Standard user but fewer than a full Administrator. Largely deprecated
in modern Windows in favor of standard/admin roles, but the group still
exists for backward compatibility.

### Authentication Concepts

**MFA (Multi-Factor Authentication)** — Requires two or more
verification methods (something you know/have/are)

**Biometrics** — Authentication using fingerprint, facial recognition,
etc.

**SSO (Single Sign-On)** — One login grants access to multiple
systems/applications

**Kerberos** — The default authentication protocol used by Windows
Active Directory. Uses time-stamped "tickets" issued by a trusted Key
Distribution Center (KDC) to verify identity without repeatedly
transmitting passwords over the network.

**TACACS+** (Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus) — A
Cisco-developed AAA protocol similar in purpose to RADIUS, but separates
authentication, authorization, and accounting into distinct processes
and encrypts the entire packet payload (RADIUS only encrypts the
password).

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** MFA combines factors from *different* categories
(password + fingerprint), not two of the same type (two passwords isn't
MFA).

## TPM 2.0 Overview

A Trusted Platform Module (TPM) is a hardware-based security root of
trust (or firmware-based equivalent) that generates, stores, and
protects cryptographic keys and records boot measurements in Platform
Configuration Registers (PCRs). TPM 2.0 is the current specification
used by modern OS security features and is required for Windows 11.

### What TPM Provides

> ● Key protection inside tamper-resistant hardware
>
> ● Hardware root of trust and measured boot attestation via PCRs
>
> ● Support for BitLocker so full-disk encryption can use the TPM
> instead of a USB startup key
>
> ● Secure storage for Windows Hello credentials and support for
> Credential Guard / Virtualization-Based Security (VBS)

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** Secure Boot and TPM are related but distinct. Secure
Boot is a UEFI feature that enforces signed boot components. TPM
measures and records boot state and enables attestation. A system can
have one without the other, though modern Windows security relies on
both together.

### TPM Versions and Implementations

**TPM 1.2** — Legacy standard; limited to SHA-1; not sufficient for
Windows 11.

**TPM 2.0** — Current standard; supports modern algorithms (SHA-256+);
required for Windows 11.

**HSM** (Hardware Security Module) — A dedicated physical device that
generates, stores, and manages cryptographic keys with tamper-resistant
hardware protection. Conceptually similar to TPM, but typically a
standalone or external device used for high-security key management at
enterprise scale.

**Firmware TPMs** — Intel PTT and AMD fTPM are firmware implementations
that satisfy the TPM 2.0 requirement when enabled.

**Microsoft Pluton** — A security processor built into the CPU. Can be
configured as the system's TPM 2.0, or run alongside a separate discrete
TPM.

### How to Verify and Enable TPM

> ● **Windows:** Run tpm.msc, or open Windows Security \> Device
> Security, to check the Specification Version and status.
>
> ● **UEFI/BIOS:** Look under Security or Advanced settings for TPM,
> PTT, fTPM, or a vendor label like "Security Chip." Enable and reboot.
>
> ● **OEM notes:** Many motherboards ship with TPM disabled by default;
> consult the manufacturer's support page.
>
> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Enabling TPM/fTPM on a system that previously had it
> disabled — or clearing the TPM — can invalidate keys tied to an
> already-active BitLocker volume, forcing entry of the 48-digit
> BitLocker recovery key on next boot. Always suspend BitLocker before
> changing TPM settings and confirm the recovery key is backed up
> beforehand.

<img src="media/image30.png" style="width:6.5in;height:6.54583in"
alt="Diagram showing TPM, Secure Boot, and Measured Boot working together in three layers on the motherboard: " />

## Password Security & Authentication

### Password Complexity Requirements

Strong password policies typically require a minimum length (commonly
8-12+ characters) and a mix of character types: uppercase, lowercase,
numbers, and special characters. Longer passwords generally provide
better security than complex-but-short ones.

#### Password Policies

**Expiration** — Forces password changes after a set period (e.g., every
90 days)

**History** — Prevents reusing recent passwords (e.g., can't reuse last
5 passwords)

**Minimum Age** — Prevents changing a password immediately after setting
it, to defeat history cycling

**Account Lockout Threshold** — Locks an account after a set number of
failed login attempts

**Lockout Duration** — How long an account stays locked before automatic
unlock (or requires admin reset)

### Password Managers

A password manager securely stores and generates unique, complex
passwords for each account, so users don't need to memorize or reuse
credentials. This reduces password reuse — one of the most common causes
of credential-based breaches.

### Single Sign-On (SSO)

SSO allows a user to authenticate once and gain access to multiple
connected systems/applications without logging in separately to each.
Common in enterprise environments using services like Active Directory
or cloud identity providers (e.g., Okta, Microsoft Entra ID).

### Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA)

Requires two or more verification factors from different categories:

> ● Something you **know** (password, PIN)
>
> ● Something you **have** (phone, hardware token, smart card)
>
> ● Something you **are** (fingerprint, facial recognition)

### Account Lockout Best Practices

> ● Set a reasonable lockout threshold (e.g., 5 failed attempts) to
> prevent brute-force attacks
>
> ● Balance security with usability — overly aggressive lockout policies
> increase help desk burden
>
> ● Combine lockout policies with MFA for stronger protection than
> either alone

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** MFA requires factors from *different* categories — two
passwords is not MFA. Also, remember: password *history* prevents reuse,
password *expiration* forces periodic changes — these are commonly
confused on exam questions.

## Quick Review — Security Fundamentals

### Questions

1.  What is the correct order of the first four steps in the CompTIA
    7-step malware removal process?

2.  A user's browser shows “Your connection is not private” on every
    HTTPS website. What should the technician check first?

3.  What is WPS, and why should it always be disabled on a SOHO router?

4.  A company laptop is stolen. The IT department confirms BitLocker was
    enabled. Is the data on the drive protected?

5.  What is the key requirement that distinguishes MFA (Multi-Factor
    Authentication) from simply requiring two passwords?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  Step 1: Identify symptoms → Step 2: Quarantine the system → Step 3:
    Disable System Restore → Step 4: Remediate (update signatures and
    scan). Steps 1–4 in exact order.

2.  Check the system date and time. SSL/TLS certificates rely on time
    validation — an incorrect clock causes every certificate to appear
    invalid.

3.  WPS (Wi-Fi Protected Setup) — it uses an 8-digit PIN that can be
    brute-forced in hours. It should always be disabled regardless of
    which security protocol (WPA2/WPA3) is in use.

4.  Yes — BitLocker provides full-disk encryption, making the data
    unreadable without the correct recovery key or TPM authentication.
    This is why BitLocker is critical for mobile devices.

5.  MFA requires authentication factors from different categories
    (something you know / something you have / something you are). Two
    passwords = two of the same category, which is NOT MFA.

# Module: Browser Security & Settings

## Module Objectives

Web browsers are the primary interface through which users access cloud
applications, email, and the internet - making them a major attack
surface. Technicians must know how to configure browser security
settings and recognize browser-based threats.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Configure essential browser security settings.
>
> ● Understand how SSL/TLS certificates protect web traffic.
>
> ● Identify and remediate browser-based security issues.
>
> ● Recognize browser-based attack vectors.

## SSL/TLS Certificates & HTTPS

When you visit a site with HTTPS, the browser verifies the site’s
SSL/TLS certificate before establishing an encrypted connection.
Understanding certificate errors is essential for both security and
helpdesk support.

**SSL/TLS Certificate** — A digital certificate issued by a Certificate
Authority (CA) that verifies a website’s identity and enables encrypted
HTTPS communication.

**Certificate Authority (CA)** — A trusted organization that issues and
validates SSL/TLS certificates. Examples: DigiCert, Let’s Encrypt,
Comodo. Browsers have a built-in list of trusted CAs.

**Certificate Error: Expired** — The certificate’s validity period has
passed. The site may be legitimate but the cert needs renewal. Warn the
user - do not dismiss this error on sites handling sensitive data.

**Certificate Error: Untrusted CA** — Issued by a CA not trusted by the
browser. Could indicate a self-signed certificate (common on internal
corporate tools) or a malicious site.

**Certificate Error: Name Mismatch** — The domain name on the
certificate doesn’t match the site being visited. Could indicate DNS
hijacking or a misconfigured server.

**Certificate Error: Wrong System Clock** — If the device’s date/time is
incorrect, all certificates appear invalid. Always check system time
first when troubleshooting SSL errors.

## Key Browser Security Settings

**Pop-up Blocker** — Prevents unsolicited pop-up windows - a common
vector for adware and scam sites. Enable by default; whitelist trusted
sites that require pop-ups.

**Private / Incognito Mode** — Does NOT save browsing history, cookies,
or form data to the local device. Does NOT make you anonymous - ISP and
employer can still see traffic. Useful for shared computers.

**Clear Browsing Data** — Removes cached files, cookies, and history.
Fixes many browser performance and display issues. Useful first
troubleshooting step for browser problems.

**Browser Extensions** — Add-ons extending browser functionality.
Security risk: malicious extensions can steal data, inject ads, or
redirect searches. Audit extensions regularly - remove unused ones.

**Password Manager** — Built-in (or third-party) tool for storing and
auto-filling passwords. Encourages use of unique, complex passwords per
site. Far more secure than reusing passwords.

**Secure DNS** — Routes DNS queries over HTTPS (DoH) or TLS (DoT) to
prevent ISP snooping and DNS hijacking. Available in Chrome, Firefox,
Edge.

**Site Permissions** — Controls per-site access to camera, microphone,
location, and notifications. Review and revoke permissions for sites
that no longer need them.

**SmartScreen / Safe Browsing** — Browser-level phishing and malware
protection. Microsoft SmartScreen (Edge/IE) and Google Safe Browsing
(Chrome) warn users before visiting known malicious sites.

## Browser-Based Attack Vectors

**Browser Hijacking** — Malware changes the browser’s homepage, default
search engine, or new tab page without consent. Fix: Remove malicious
extensions, run malware scan, reset browser settings.

**Drive-By Download** — Malware automatically downloads when a user
visits a compromised or malicious website - no clicking required.
Defense: Keep browser and plugins updated, enable SmartScreen/Safe
Browsing.

**Malicious Extensions** — Fake or compromised browser add-ons that
steal credentials, inject ads, or redirect traffic. Defense: Only
install extensions from official stores, review permissions before
installing.

**Cross-Site Scripting (XSS)** — Attacker injects malicious scripts into
a trusted website that then execute in the victim’s browser. Defense:
Keep browser updated; browser-level XSS filters provide some protection.

**Clickjacking** — A transparent malicious layer is placed over a
legitimate page, tricking users into clicking hidden elements. Defense:
Browser X-Frame-Options headers prevent most clickjacking.

**Fake Certificate Warnings** — Scammers display fake browser security
alerts (not real browser warnings) to trick users into calling a phone
number or downloading software. Defense: Real browser warnings come from
the browser itself - not pop-up windows.

# Module: SOHO Network Security

## Module Objectives

Small Office/Home Office (SOHO) networks are among the most common
environments A+ technicians support. Securing these networks requires
configuring routers, access points, and connected devices correctly -
many of which ship with dangerously insecure default settings.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Identify and remediate common SOHO network security vulnerabilities.
>
> ● Configure router and wireless security settings correctly.
>
> ● Set up guest network isolation.
>
> ● Understand firewall configuration basics for SOHO environments.

## Common SOHO Security Mistakes

**Using default admin credentials** — CRITICAL. Default passwords
(admin/admin, admin/password) are published online. Anyone on the
network can access the router admin interface. Fix: Change admin
username and password immediately on setup.

**Using default SSID** — The default SSID (e.g., “NETGEAR-5G” or
“Linksys”) reveals the router model, helping attackers target known
vulnerabilities. Fix: Change to a non-identifying SSID.

**Using WEP or WPA (old security)** — WEP can be cracked in minutes. WPA
(original) has known vulnerabilities. Fix: Use WPA2-AES or WPA3
exclusively.

**WPS enabled** — Wi-Fi Protected Setup has a known PIN brute-force
vulnerability. The 8-digit PIN can be cracked in hours. Fix: Disable WPS
entirely.

**Remote management enabled** — Allows access to the router admin
interface from the internet - a direct attack vector. Fix: Disable
remote management unless specifically needed.

**Outdated router firmware** — Unpatched firmware contains known
exploits. Fix: Enable automatic firmware updates or check monthly for
updates.

**No guest network** — Visitors and IoT devices on the main network can
potentially access shared resources. Fix: Create a separate guest
VLAN/network for visitors and untrusted devices.

## SOHO Router Security Checklist

> ● Change the default admin username and password to something strong
> and unique.
>
> ● Update router firmware to the latest version. Enable auto-updates if
> available.
>
> ● Set wireless security to WPA3 or WPA2-AES. Disable WEP, WPA (TKIP),
> and WPS.
>
> ● Change the default SSID to something that does not identify the
> router brand or household.
>
> ● Create a separate guest network for visitors and IoT devices with
> network isolation enabled.
>
> ● Disable remote management (WAN access to router admin panel).
>
> ● Disable UPnP (Universal Plug and Play) - it can be exploited to open
> ports automatically.
>
> ● Enable the built-in firewall (SPI firewall) if not already active.
>
> ● Review port forwarding rules - remove any that are no longer needed.
>
> ● Change the default LAN IP range if possible (e.g., from 192.168.1.x
> to 10.0.x.x) to reduce automated attack scripts.

## Guest Network Configuration

A properly configured guest network is one of the most important
security measures for SOHO environments. It isolates visitors and
untrusted devices (smart TVs, IoT gadgets, personal phones) from the
main network where sensitive data and shared resources live.

**Network Isolation (Client Isolation)** — Prevents devices on the guest
network from communicating with each other or with devices on the main
network. Guests can only reach the internet.

**Separate SSID & Password** — Guest network has its own name and
password. Main network credentials are never shared with guests.

**Bandwidth Limiting** — Optionally limits how much bandwidth guest
devices can consume, preventing a guest from saturating the connection.

**IoT Device Segmentation** — Smart home devices (cameras, speakers,
thermostats) should be on the guest/IoT network - not the main network
with computers and phones containing sensitive data.

## SOHO Firewall Configuration

**SPI (Stateful Packet Inspection) Firewall** — Built into most consumer
routers. Tracks the state of active connections and blocks unsolicited
inbound traffic. Should always be enabled.

**Port Forwarding** — Opens specific ports to allow inbound connections
to a specific internal device (e.g., game server, NAS). Only open ports
that are actively needed - close them when done.

**DMZ (Demilitarized Zone)** — Places a single device completely outside
the firewall, exposing all ports to the internet. Very risky - use only
for devices that genuinely need it and accept the security implications.

**Inbound vs. Outbound Rules** — Most SOHO firewalls only filter inbound
traffic by default. Consider adding outbound filtering to block known
malicious domains and restrict which applications can reach the
internet.

> **✔ Pro Tip:** WPS is always a vulnerability - disable it regardless
> of the security protocol in use. - Default credentials = instant
> compromise. Always change them first. - Guest network + client
> isolation = visitors and IoT devices can’t reach your main
> computers. - UPnP is convenient but dangerous - disable it in
> security-conscious environments. - DMZ exposes a device to the entire
> internet with no firewall protection - use with extreme caution.

# Module: Mobile Device Management (MDM)

## Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Explain the purpose and role of MDM in a corporate environment.
>
> ● Differentiate between Corporate and BYOD device ownership models.
>
> ● Identify common MDM features such as remote wipe, app control, and
> geofencing.
>
> ● Recognize how MDM integrates with security, networking, and
> operational procedures.

## What is MDM?

Mobile Device Management is a centralized system used by organizations
to manage smartphones, tablets, laptops, and BYOD/Corporate-owned
devices. It allows IT administrators to push updates, enforce security
policies, and monitor device status.

## Device Ownership Models

**Corporate-Owned Devices**\
Definition: Devices purchased and supplied by the company for employee
use.\
MDM Control Level: Full Control. MDM can enforce strict policies,
install/remove apps, and fully wipe the device.

**Bring Your Own Device (BYOD)**\
Definition: Employees use their personal mobile devices to access
corporate resources.\
MDM Control Level: Limited Control. MDM uses MAM, focusing only on
securing corporate data within a container on the device.

## Key MDM Features

**Remote Wipe** — Erases all data on a lost or stolen device (Full Wipe)
or only corporate data (Selective Wipe for BYOD).

**Remote Lock** — Locks device to prevent unauthorized access of a
misplaced or stolen device.

**App Whitelisting** — Only approved apps can run on the device.

**App Blacklisting** — Blocks specific apps (e.g., Dropbox, risky social
apps).

**Geofencing** — Creates virtual boundaries that trigger security
actions based on the device’s physical location.

**VPN Enforcement** — Forces encrypted tunnels for corporate apps and
data.

**Certificate Deployment** — Uses device certificates for secure
authentication to Wi-Fi, VPN, and internal resources.

**Configuration Profiles** — Enforce Wi-Fi settings, VPN, email setup,
password policies, and feature restrictions.

## Privacy Management

#### (What MDM Can vs. Cannot See on BYOD)

**Can:** Device model & serial number — **Cannot:** Personal photos

**Can:** OS version & security patch level — **Cannot:** Personal text
messages or phone call logs

**Can:** Whether the device is encrypted — **Cannot:** Personal email
accounts

**Can:** Installed work apps — **Cannot:** Browser history

**Can:** Compliance status & jailbreak/root status — **Cannot:**
Personal app data or location history

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Full vs Selective Wipe → Full = all data erased
> (lost/stolen corporate device). Selective = only corporate data
> removed (BYOD). BYOD Privacy → MDM cannot see personal content; only
> corporate container. Geofencing → Location‑based enforcement is a
> common exam scenario.

# Module: Remote Access Technologies

## Module Objectives

Remote access technologies allow technicians, administrators, and end
users to connect to systems from a different physical location. This is
a standalone Core 2 objective covering a range of tools from basic
desktop sharing to enterprise remote management platforms.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Identify and differentiate between common remote access methods.
>
> ● Understand when to use each tool based on the scenario.
>
> ● Recognize security best practices for remote access.
>
> ● Explain the difference between user-initiated remote access and
> IT-managed remote monitoring.

## Remote Desktop Technologies

**RDP (Remote Desktop Protocol)** — Microsoft’s built-in protocol for
graphical remote access to Windows systems. Port 3389. Provides full
desktop experience. Should always be protected behind a VPN - never
expose RDP directly to the internet.

**VNC (Virtual Network Computing)** — Cross-platform screen-sharing
protocol. Shares the actual display (rather than creating a new session
like RDP). Common tools: TightVNC, RealVNC, TigerVNC. Port 5900.

**SPICE (Simple Protocol for Independent Computing Environments)** —
High-performance remote display protocol used primarily in virtual
machine environments (e.g., Red Hat Virtualization, KVM VMs). Supports
audio, video, and USB redirection.

**Apple Remote Desktop** — macOS-specific remote management tool. Allows
screen sharing, file transfer, and remote command execution on Mac
systems.

## Secure Remote Access Methods

**VPN (Virtual Private Network)** — Creates an encrypted tunnel between
a remote device and the corporate network. Allows access to internal
resources as if physically on-site.

**SSH (Secure Shell)** — Encrypted command-line remote access. Port 22.
Primary tool for remote administration of Linux/Unix servers and network
devices. Replaces insecure Telnet (port 23).

**SFTP (SSH File Transfer Protocol)** — Secure file transfer over an SSH
connection. Port 22. Used to securely upload/download files on remote
servers. Replaces insecure FTP.

**Zero Trust Network Access (ZTNA)** — Modern replacement for VPN that
grants access to specific applications (not the whole network) based on
identity and device compliance, regardless of location.

## IT Remote Management Tools

**RMM (Remote Monitoring and Management)** — Enterprise platforms
allowing MSPs and IT teams to monitor, manage, patch, and troubleshoot
endpoints remotely without user interaction.

**WinRM (Windows Remote Management)** — Microsoft’s command-line remote
management service based on WS-Management protocol. Enables PowerShell
remoting and remote script execution on Windows systems.

**IPMI (Intelligent Platform Management Interface)** — Out-of-band
management interface built into server motherboards. Allows remote power
control, console access, and hardware monitoring even when the OS is
offline.

**iDRAC / iLO / IMM** — Vendor-specific out-of-band management: Dell
iDRAC, HP iLO, IBM IMM. Allow remote server management independent of
the OS.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** RDP Port 3389, never exposed directly; always behind
> VPN. **RDP** creates new session; **VNC** shares existing display.
> **Out‑of‑band management** → IPMI/iDRAC/iLO/IMM work even if OS is
> offline. SSH encrypted, Telnet insecure. SFTP secure, FTP insecure.

## Sharing & Collaboration Tools

**TeamViewer** — Popular cross-platform remote support and
screen-sharing tool. Does not require VPN — connects through
TeamViewer’s cloud relay. Widely used for remote helpdesk support.

**AnyDesk** — Remote desktop tool similar to TeamViewer. Known for
low-latency performance. Used for both IT support and remote work.

**Microsoft Quick Assist** — Built into Windows 10/11. Allows screen
sharing and remote control for IT support sessions. Requires both
parties to be signed into Microsoft accounts.

**Chrome Remote Desktop** — Free Google tool allowing remote access
through a Chrome browser. Cross-platform. Good for basic remote support.

**Videoconferencing (Zoom, Teams, Meet)** — While not traditional remote
access tools, screen sharing via videoconferencing is frequently used in
helpdesk scenarios for guided troubleshooting.

## Remote Access Security

> ● Never expose RDP (port 3389) or VNC (port 5900) directly to the
> internet - always use a VPN or SSH tunnel.
>
> ● Use multi-factor authentication (MFA) for all remote access
> solutions.
>
> ● Implement principle of least privilege - remote users should only
> access what they need.
>
> ● Use jump servers (bastion hosts) as a single controlled entry point
> for remote access to internal networks.
>
> ● Monitor and log all remote access sessions for security auditing.
>
> ● Disable remote access services when not in use to reduce attack
> surface.
>
> ● Keep remote access software updated - vulnerable RDP/VNC instances
> are a common ransomware entry point.
>
> **✔ Pro Tip:** VNC = cross-platform screen sharing, port 5900. - RMM =
> IT management platform for monitoring and managing many endpoints
> remotely. - WinRM enables PowerShell remoting on Windows systems. -
> SPICE is used for virtual machine remote display, not general desktop
> access.

## Quick Review — Network & Mobile Security

### Questions

1.  What is the difference between a full wipe and a selective wipe in
    MDM, and when would each be used?

2.  Why should RDP (port 3389) never be exposed directly to the
    internet, and what should protect it?

3.  A SOHO router still uses the default admin credentials
    (admin/admin). What is the primary security risk, and what is the
    fix?

4.  A company wants to allow guests to use Wi-Fi without giving them
    access to internal file shares and printers. What router feature
    should be configured?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  Full wipe erases ALL data on a lost/stolen corporate-owned device.
    Selective wipe removes only corporate data/apps while leaving
    personal data intact — used for BYOD devices when an employee leaves
    the company.

2.  Exposed RDP is one of the most common ransomware entry points.
    Attackers scan for open port 3389 and brute-force credentials.
    Always protect RDP behind a VPN or SSH tunnel — never expose it
    directly to the public internet.

3.  Anyone on the network can access the router admin interface using
    the publicly-known default credentials, allowing them to change
    Wi-Fi passwords, view connected devices, or redirect traffic. Fix:
    Change the admin username and password immediately on first setup.

4.  Configure a guest network with client isolation (also called AP
    isolation) enabled. This allows guests internet access while
    preventing them from communicating with devices on the main network.

# Module: Windows Recovery Environment (WinRE)

## Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Access the Windows Recovery Environment using multiple methods.
>
> ● Identify and use each recovery tool available within WinRE.
>
> ● Differentiate between Startup Repair, System Restore, System Image
> Recovery, and Reset This PC.
>
> ● Understand Safe Mode options and when to use each variant.
>
> ● Apply the correct recovery method based on the failure scenario.

## What is WinRE?

WinRE (Windows Recovery Environment) is a minimal operating environment
built into Windows 10/11 that provides diagnostic and repair tools when
the main OS cannot start normally. It runs from a hidden recovery
partition.

## How to Access WinRE

**From within Windows** — Settings, System, Recovery, Advanced Startup,
Restart Now

**Shift + Restart** — Hold Shift while clicking Restart (Start Menu or
Lock Screen)

**Command Line** — shutdown /r /o /t 0

**Automatic Trigger** — Windows enters WinRE after two consecutive
failed boot attempts

**From Installation Media** — Boot from USB/DVD, select language, click
“Repair your computer”

## WinRE Recovery Tools

**Startup Repair**\
Purpose: Automatically fixes common boot problems (missing boot files,
BCD corruption)\
Data Loss?: No

**Startup Settings**\
Purpose: Access Safe Mode, boot logging, disable driver signature
enforcement\
Data Loss?: No

**System Restore**\
Purpose: Returns system files/registry to a previous point in time\
Data Loss?: No (personal files safe; apps after point removed)

**System Image Recovery**\
Purpose: Restores entire system from previously created image backup\
Data Loss?: Overwrites current drive

**Command Prompt**\
Purpose: Manual repair commands (bootrec, sfc, DISM, chkdsk, diskpart)\
Data Loss?: Depends on commands used

**Uninstall Updates**\
Purpose: Removes most recent quality or feature update\
Data Loss?: No

**UEFI Firmware Settings**\
Purpose: Reboots directly into UEFI/BIOS setup\
Data Loss?: No

## Safe Mode Options

**Safe Mode** — Essential drivers only. No networking. Diagnose
driver/software conflicts.

**Safe Mode with Networking** — Includes network drivers and internet.
Download fixes or run cloud AV scans.

**Safe Mode with Command Prompt** — Command prompt only (no desktop
GUI). Advanced repair when desktop won’t load.

**Enable Boot Logging** — Creates ntbtlog.txt listing every driver
loaded. Identify which driver causes failure.

**How to reach Safe Mode:** WinRE, Troubleshoot, Advanced Options,
Startup Settings, Restart, then press 4 (F4) for Safe Mode, 5 (F5) for
Safe Mode with Networking, or 6 (F6) for Command Prompt.

**Important:** F8 does NOT reliably work in Windows 10/11. WinRE is the
official path.

**Safe Mode diagnostic logic:** If the problem disappears in Safe Mode,
the cause is a third-party driver, service, or startup program. If the
problem persists in Safe Mode, the issue is in Windows core files or
hardware.

## Reset This PC

**Keep my files**\
What It Does: Reinstalls Windows, removes all apps, keeps personal
files\
Files: Kept\
Apps: Removed\
OS: Reset

**Remove everything**\
What It Does: Wipes drive and reinstalls Windows from scratch\
Files: Deleted\
Apps: Removed\
OS: Reset

Reinstallation Source Options:

> ● Cloud Download: Downloads fresh image from Microsoft (~4 GB). Use
> when local image may be corrupted.
>
> ● Local Reinstall: Uses recovery image on device. Faster, works
> offline.

**“Clean the drive” option:** When choosing “Remove everything,” enables
basic data overwrite. Use when giving away or reassigning device. Not a
substitute for proper data destruction on regulated data.

## WinRE Command Prompt Reference

**bootrec /fixmbr** — Writes new Master Boot Record

**bootrec /fixboot** — Writes new boot sector

**bootrec /rebuildbcd** — Rebuilds Boot Configuration Data store

**sfc /scannow /offbootdir=C:\\ /offwindir=C:\Windows** — Repairs
corrupted Windows system files offline mode)

**DISM /Image:C:\\Cleanup-Image /RestoreHealth** — Repairs Windows image
from WinRE

**chkdsk C: /f /r /x** — Checks and repairs disk errors and bad sectors

**bcdedit** — Views or modifies boot configuration

## Recovery Method Decision Matrix

**Won’t boot after Windows Update**\
First Try: Startup Repair, then Uninstall Updates\
If That Fails: System Restore

**Won’t boot after driver install**\
First Try: Safe Mode, roll back driver\
If That Fails: System Restore

**Corrupted system files**\
First Try: sfc /scannow, then DISM\
If That Fails: System Restore

**“bootmgr is missing” or BCD error**\
First Try: Startup Repair\
If That Fails: bootrec commands from Command Prompt

**Malware preventing normal operation**\
First Try: Safe Mode, malware scan\
If That Fails: Reset (Remove everything)

**Complete drive failure (new drive)**\
First Try: System Image Recovery\
If That Fails: Clean install from media

**Preparing PC for new user**\
First Try: Reset (Remove everything + Clean the drive)\
If That Fails: N/A

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** Startup Repair is always the FIRST tool to try when
Windows won’t boot. Safe Mode isolates problems. Problem gone =
third-party cause. Problem persists = core OS or hardware. System
Restore does NOT affect personal files. Applications installed after the
restore point WILL be removed. F8 does NOT work in Windows 10/11 for
Safe Mode. Use Shift+Restart or WinRE automatic trigger. System Image
Recovery requires a pre-existing image. If none exists, this option is
unavailable. The WinRE Command Prompt runs with SYSTEM privileges (no
admin elevation needed). “bootmgr is missing” = use bootrec commands.
BSOD after update = Uninstall Updates.

# Module: Workstation Backup & Recovery

## Module Objectives

Data loss from hardware failure, ransomware, accidental deletion, or
disasters is a constant risk. A+ technicians must understand backup
strategies, types, schedules, and recovery procedures to protect
organizational data.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Identify and explain backup types: full, incremental, and
> differential.
>
> ● Design appropriate backup schedules for given scenarios.
>
> ● Understand on-site, off-site, and cloud backup strategies.
>
> ● Use Windows and macOS built-in backup tools.
>
> ● Execute basic recovery procedures.

## Backup Types

**Full Backup**\
What It Backs Up: Everything - all selected files and folders regardless
of whether they’ve changed.\
Speed: Slowest to create\
Recovery Complexity: Simplest - restore from the single full backup.

**Incremental Backup**\
What It Backs Up: Only files changed since the LAST backup (full OR
incremental).\
Speed: Fastest to create\
Recovery Complexity: Most complex - need the last full + every
incremental since.

**Differential Backup**\
What It Backs Up: All files changed since the last FULL backup
(regardless of differentials).\
Speed: Medium\
Recovery Complexity: Simpler than incremental - need last full + latest
differential only.

**Synthetic Full**\
What It Backs Up: Combines a previous full + all incrementals into a new
full backup without running a new full backup against the source.\
Speed: Fast (server-side)\
Recovery Complexity: Same as full - single restore point.

## Backup Schedules

**Full Weekly + Daily Incrementals** — Full backup every weekend;
incremental backups each weekday. Balances backup speed with recovery
complexity. Most common enterprise strategy.

**Full Weekly + Daily Differentials** — Full backup every weekend;
differential backups each weekday. Slower backup than incremental
strategy but faster and simpler recovery.

**Continuous / Real-Time Backup** — Changes are backed up as they
happen. Used for critical systems where minimal data loss is acceptable.
Examples: database transaction logs, VSS shadow copies.

**GFS (Grandfather-Father-Son)** — A rotation scheme keeping daily
(son), weekly (father), and monthly (grandfather) backups. Provides
multiple recovery points at different time scales.

## Backup Storage Locations

**On-Site (Local)** — Fast backup and recovery. No internet dependency.
Risk: destroyed in the same disaster (fire, flood) as the primary data.

**Off-Site (Physical)** — Protected from on-site disasters. Tapes/drives
physically transported to a secure location. Slow recovery if media must
be shipped.

**Cloud Backup** — Protected from local disasters. Accessible from
anywhere. Scalable. Cons: dependent on internet speed for backup and
recovery; ongoing cost.

**3-2-1 Rule (Best Practice)** — 3 copies of data, on 2 different media
types, with 1 copy off-site. Gold standard backup strategy.

## Windows Backup Tools

**File History** — Automatically backs up personal files (Documents,
Pictures, Desktop, etc.) to an external drive or network location at
regular intervals. Not a full system backup.

**Windows Backup (System Image)** — Creates a complete image of the
entire drive including OS, applications, and data. Can restore the full
system to a previous state. Available in Control Panel \> Backup and
Restore.

**System Restore** — Creates restore points of Windows system files and
registry. Does NOT back up personal files. Used to undo problematic
driver installations or system changes.

**OneDrive Sync** — Syncs designated folders to Microsoft’s cloud.
Provides cloud backup for files in synced folders. Not a full backup
solution but protects against local file loss.

**Reset This PC** — Reinstalls Windows from a recovery image. Option to
keep or remove personal files. Does not restore applications. Last
resort for severe OS issues.

**WBAdmin (Command Line)** — Windows Server Backup command-line tool.
Can create full system backups and bare-metal recovery images from the
command prompt.

## macOS Backup Tools

**Time Machine** — Apple’s built-in backup solution. Backs up to an
external drive or network location hourly (keeping 24 hours), daily
(keeping a month), and weekly (keeping all previous months). Supports
file-level and full system restore.

**iCloud Drive** — Syncs Desktop and Documents folders to iCloud.
Protects against local hardware failure but not a complete backup.

**Migration Assistant** — Transfers data, applications, and settings
from an old Mac to a new one via Time Machine backup, direct connection,
or network.

> **✔ Pro Tip:** Incremental = backs up changes since last ANY backup.
> Faster to back up, slower to restore. - Differential = backs up
> changes since last FULL backup. Slower to back up, faster to
> restore. - 3-2-1 rule: 3 copies, 2 different media types, 1 off-site.
> Memorize this. - System Restore does NOT protect personal files - only
> system files and registry. - File History protects personal files -
> NOT the OS or applications. - A system image is the only tool that
> lets you restore an entire Windows installation.

## Quick Review— Recovery & Backup

### Questions

1.  What is the difference between an incremental backup and a
    differential backup in terms of what each backs up and restore
    complexity?

2.  What is the 3-2-1 backup rule?

3.  A user deletes an important file. The IT department has File History
    enabled. Can the file be recovered?

4.  What is the only Windows tool that can restore an entire system
    installation (OS + applications + data)?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  Incremental backs up files changed since the LAST backup (full or
    incremental) — fastest to create but slowest to restore (need last
    full + every incremental). Differential backs up all files changed
    since the last FULL backup — slower to create but simpler to restore
    (need last full + latest differential only).

2.  3 copies of data, on 2 different media types, with 1 copy off-site.
    This is the gold standard backup strategy that protects against
    hardware failure, site disasters, and human error.

3.  Yes — File History maintains versioned backups of personal files in
    Documents, Pictures, Desktop, etc. The user can browse to the file
    location, right-click, select “Restore previous versions,” and
    recover the deleted file.

4.  System Image Backup (also called Windows Backup) — creates a
    complete image of the entire drive including OS, applications, and
    data. System Restore does NOT protect personal files, and File
    History does NOT protect the OS.

# Module: Troubleshooting Methodology & Procedures

## Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● List and apply the six steps of the systematic troubleshooting
> methodology.
>
> ● Explain the importance of documentation in the troubleshooting
> process.
>
> ● Recognize the procedural requirement to back up data before
> implementing any solution.

## The 6-Step Troubleshooting Method

Memorizing this exact sequence is mandatory. CompTIA will routinely
present a scenario where a technician has just completed one of these
actions and will ask you, *“What should the technician do next?”*

**Step 1** — Identify the Problem: Question the user to find out what
recent changes were made. Check event logs and system alerts. Crucial:
Always perform a full system and data backup before you make any changes
to the system!

**Step 2** — Establish a Theory of Probable Cause: Start by questioning
the obvious (e.g., “Is the device actually plugged in?”). Use the OSI
model (top-to-bottom or bottom-to-top) to systematically narrow down the
issue. If necessary, conduct internal/external research (knowledge
bases, manufacturer websites).

**Step 3** — Test the Theory to Determine Cause: Try a quick,
non-destructive test to see if your theory is correct.\
• If confirmed: Move to Step 4.\
• If incorrect: Go back to Step 2 and establish a new theory.\
• If out of your depth: Escalate the ticket to a higher-level
technician.

**Step 4** — Establish a Plan of Action & Implement: Write out the steps
needed to fix the issue. Consider potential negative impacts or
corporate downtime (e.g., “Will restarting this switch disconnect the
entire floor?”). Once the plan is safe, execute the repair.

**Step 5** — Verify Functionality & Implement Preventive Measures: Do
not just assume the fix worked. Have the user independently confirm that
their workflow is restored. Once verified, implement preventative
measures (e.g., installing a surge protector, updating policies) so the
issue doesn’t happen again.

**Step 6** — Document Findings, Actions, and Outcomes: The job is not
done until the paperwork is finished. Update the helpdesk ticketing
system with a clear record of the initial symptoms, the root cause, and
the exact steps taken to resolve it. This builds the corporate knowledge
base for future technicians.

<img src="media/image31.png" style="width:6.46761in;height:8.36619in"
alt="CompTIA 6-step troubleshooting methodology flowchart, showing decision points for theory confirmation and resolution verification." />

## Data Backups Requirement

Before a technician ever opens a computer case, changes a registry key,
or runs a malware removal tool, they must secure the user’s data.
CompTIA enforces this as a strict procedural requirement in **Step 1:
Identify the Problem**.

Why is this non-negotiable?

> ● **Mitigating Catastrophic Loss:** Troubleshooting is inherently
> risky. A simple fix (like rebuilding a boot partition or running a
> system restore) can unexpectedly corrupt a drive. If the data is
> backed up, a broken OS is just a minor inconvenience. If it isn’t
> backed up, it is a catastrophic business failure.
>
> ● **Liability and Accountability:** In corporate environments, user
> data often contains critical financial records, legal documents, or
> proprietary assets. Backing up data protects the IT department from
> liability if the hardware fails during the repair process.

### Step 6 Deep Dive: The Importance of Documentation

The phrase to memorize for the A+ exam is: *“The job is not done until
the paperwork is finished”*. Documentation is the final, essential step
of the troubleshooting methodology.

Why Documentation is Critical:

> ● **Building the Corporate Knowledge Base (KB):** When you document a
> complex fix, you ensure the next technician who faces the same issue
> doesn’t have to start from scratch. This drastically reduces future
> ticket resolution times.
>
> ● **Identifying Recurring Problems:** Helpdesk software tracks
> documented tickets. If ten users report a broken printer connection in
> one week, proper documentation allows IT management to identify a
> systemic network issue rather than treating it as ten isolated
> incidents.
>
> ● **SLA and CYA:** Documentation provides a legal and procedural paper
> trail. It proves you met your Service Level Agreement (SLA) response
> times and protects you (Cover Your Assets) if a user claims a repair
> wasn’t completed properly.

### What to Include in a Perfect Ticket Document

> ● **The Initial Symptoms** — Exactly what the user reported (e.g.,
> “Monitor flashing blue when opening CAD software”).
>
> ● **The Root Cause** — What actually caused the issue (e.g., “Outdated
> NVIDIA display drivers”).
>
> ● **The Exact Steps Taken** — A clear record of the exact steps taken
> to resolve it (e.g., “Uninstalled v1.2, installed v1.5 from
> manufacturer website, rebooted”).
>
> ● **The Preventative Measures** — How you ensured it won’t happen
> again (e.g., “Enabled automatic driver updates in Group Policy”).
>
> **✔ Pro Tip:** Memorize the 6 steps of the troubleshooting
> methodology - these apply to software and hardware. - Know the
> difference between Task Manager and Resource Monitor. - Compatibility
> mode is the first thing to try for legacy application issues. - BSOD
> error codes are in Event Viewer → Windows Logs → System. - On mobile
> devices: soft reset → settings reset → factory reset is the correct
> escalation path.

## Quick Review — Troubleshooting Methodology

### Questions

1.  In the CompTIA 6-step troubleshooting methodology, what must a
    technician do BEFORE making any changes to a system?

2.  A technician establishes a theory (bad RAM) and tests it, but the
    problem persists. According to the 6-step process, what should the
    technician do next?

3.  Which fire extinguisher class is most important for IT environments,
    and what does it cover?

4.  A technician fixes a user's printer issue but does not document the
    resolution in the ticketing system. Why is this a problem?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  Back up the user's data. This is a mandatory procedural requirement
    in Step 1 (Identify the Problem) before any changes are made to the
    system.

2.  Return to Step 2 and establish a new theory of probable cause. If
    the theory is confirmed incorrect during testing, loop back and
    develop a different theory rather than proceeding forward.

3.  Class C — electrical fires. Most important for IT environments
    because data centers and server rooms contain energized equipment.
    Use CO2 or dry chemical extinguishers, never water.

4.  The job is not done until documentation is complete. Without
    documentation, future technicians cannot learn from this fix, and
    the organization cannot identify recurring systemic problems.
    Documentation builds the corporate knowledge base.

# Module: Physical Safety & Environmental Controls

Technicians must understand proper safety procedures, environmental
requirements, and facility controls to protect both equipment and
personnel.

## Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Identify fire extinguisher classes and when to use each type in an
> IT environment.
>
> ● Apply the PASS method for operating a fire extinguisher.
>
> ● Understand environmental requirements (temperature, humidity,
> airflow) for IT facilities.
>
> ● Recognize ESD hazards and proper prevention techniques.
>
> ● Follow safe handling procedures for toner, batteries, and other
> IT-specific materials.

## Environmental Standards

**Temperature** — 18–27°C (64–80°F) for server rooms.

**Humidity** — 40–60%. Lower humidity increases ESD risk; higher
humidity increases condensation risk.

**Airflow & Filtration** — Use HVAC redundancy (N+1 or higher). Maintain
cold aisle / hot aisle alignment.

## Fire Safety Essentials

**Class A**\
Use For: Wood, paper, cloth\
Notes: Common combustibles.

**Class B**\
Use For: Flammable liquids\
Notes: Grease, gasoline, solvents.

**Class C**\
Use For: Electrical fires\
Notes: Most important for IT environments.

**Class D**\
Use For: Combustible metals\
Notes: Industrial settings.

**Class K**\
Use For: Cooking oils & fats\
Notes: Commercial kitchens.

### **PASS Method**

When operating a fire extinguisher, remember: **P** - Pull the pin.
**A** - Aim at the base of the fire. **S** - Squeeze the handle. **S** -
Sweep side-to-side.

<img src="media/image32.png" style="width:6.5in;height:6.85417in"
alt="Fire safety essentials infographic defining classes A, B, C, D, and K, with the PASS method mnemonic for using fire extinguishers." />

## Additional Safety Guidelines

> ● Toner Safety: Use cold water for toner spills on skin - hot water
> melts and embeds toner.
>
> ● Battery Disposal: Batteries (especially Li-ion / Li-poly) must be
> recycled, not trashed.
>
> ● ESD Safety: Avoid household vacuum cleaners - they generate static.
> Use ESD-safe vacuums. Wear ESD wrist straps and use ESD mats when
> handling components.

# Module: Customer Service & Ticket Lifecycle

## Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Demonstrate effective and professional communication skills with end
> users.
>
> ● Follow the complete help desk ticket lifecycle from intake to
> resolution.
>
> ● Identify prioritization methods (severity, impact, urgency).

## Communication Essentials

Technical brilliance won’t save a ticket if you can’t communicate
effectively with a frustrated user. This section breaks down the
essential soft skills - from active listening to managing expectations -
required to navigate high-stress helpdesk scenarios. CompTIA heavily
tests these concepts on the Core 2 (220-1202) exam, making your IT
“bedside manner” just as critical to passing as your hardware knowledge!

### Tone and Word Choice

Words dictate the trajectory of a customer interaction. Effective word
choice builds cooperation, de-escalates conflict, and ultimately saves
valuable troubleshooting time. Whether communicating verbally or via a
helpdesk ticketing system, a technician must always use language that is
**confident, professional, and positive**.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** You are highly likely to encounter a PBQ that
> simulates a live chat window with a very angry user. You will be given
> a drop-down menu of potential responses. Always choose the option that
> acknowledges the user’s frustration, avoids blaming them, and
> immediately offers a collaborative solution - just like the right-hand
> column of the table above!

### Professional Translation Matrix

**Negative:** “You did it wrong.” —**Positive:** “Let’s walk through the
steps together.”

**Negative:** “That’s not my department.” — **Positive:** “I’ll connect
you with the right resource and follow up.”

**Negative:** “Calm down.” —**Positive:** “I understand this is
frustrating. I’m here to help.”

**Negative:** “I don’t know.” —**Positive:** “Let me find out for you.”

**Negative:** “You have to…” — **Positive:** “To fix this, here is what
I will need from you…”

**Negative:** “Who is this?” —**Positive:** “May I ask who is calling?”

### Verbal vs. Written Communication Standards

CompTIA expects technicians to adapt their communication style based on
the medium they are using. You must master the specific rules for both
verbal and written support:

> ● **Verbal Communication (Phone/In-Person):** You must control your
> **pace, inflection, tone, and volume**. Speaking too quickly can
> overwhelm a user, while a flat, monotone inflection can make you sound
> dismissive or bored.
>
> ● **Written Literacy (Email/Live Chat):** Without the benefit of vocal
> tone, written text can easily be misinterpreted. You must utilize
> proper **grammar, spelling, sentence structure, and vocabulary**.
> Never use text-message shorthand (e.g., “BRB,” “U R”), and strictly
> avoid drowning the user in highly technical jargon or “acronym soup.”

## Ticket Lifecycle

Every support interaction in a help desk environment follows a
structured path from the moment a user reports a problem to the moment
the ticket is officially closed. Understanding this lifecycle ensures
consistent, professional service and complete documentation - both
critical for CompTIA A+ exam scenarios and real-world IT work.

**Step 1. Ticket Creation (Intake)** — Username, contact info,
device/asset ID, problem description, date/time, affected system.

**Step 2. Categorization & Prioritization** — P1 Critical (Outage), P2
High (Major function impaired), P3 Medium (Non-critical), P4 Low (Minor
inconvenience).

**Step 3. Troubleshooting & Documentation** — User Q&A, testing, logs,
system tools. Document steps taken, tools used, error messages,
findings.

**Step 4. Escalation (If Needed)** — When issue exceeds permissions,
specialized knowledge required, or SLA time is at risk.

**Step 5. Resolution & Implementation** — Apply fix, install updates,
restore service, complete configuration change.

**Step 6. Verification of Functionality** — Confirm issue fully
resolved. User confirmation required.

**Step 7. User Education (If Applicable)** — Explain how to avoid future
issues. Provide documentation.

**Step 8. Ticket Closure** — Closed only after user confirms solution
and all documentation is entered.

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** The A+ exam frequently presents ticket lifecycle
questions as ordering tasks - drag-and-drop or sequencing scenarios
where you must arrange steps in the correct order. The two most commonly
misplaced steps are User Education (step 7 - after resolution, not
before) and Ticket Closure (step 8 - always last, always requires user
confirmation first). Memorize the 8-step sequence cold.

<img src="media/image33.png" style="width:6.49931in;height:6.78822in"
alt="A flowchart detailing an 8-step ticket lifecycle from intake to closure." />

**ℹ️ Exam Tip:** Remember the three ticket metrics using **SIU**:
Severity = How badly is the system technically broken? Impact = Who and
how many are affected? Urgency = When does the business need this
resolved? Note: SIU helps you remember the three definitions but **only
Impact + Urgency actually calculate ticket Priority.** Severity
describes damage, not urgency to fix.

### Prioritization Methods

Technicians use a structured prioritization matrix to objectively
calculate a ticket’s priority.

**Severity**\
*The Definition:* The Technical Degree of Failure. How badly is the
specific hardware or software broken, regardless of its business use?\
*Real-World Example*: A totally fried motherboard is a High Severity
hardware failure. However, if that motherboard is in a spare laptop in
the storage closet, the overall ticket priority remains low.

**Impact**\
*The Definition:* The Scope of the Damage. How much of the business is
affected? Does this halt one user, an entire department, or the whole
company’s revenue stream?\
*Real-World Example*: A single broken printer in a cubicle has Low
Impact. The main switch failing in the server room has High Impact.

**Urgency**\
*The Definition:* The Time Sensitivity. How quickly will the business
feel the pain, and is there a viable workaround available?\
*Real-World Example:* A payroll server failing on a Sunday has Low
Urgency (nobody needs it until Friday). A payroll server failing on
Friday morning has High Urgency.

### The Priority Calculation

Helpdesk software automatically calculates the final Ticket Priority (P1
through P4) by combining **Impact** and **Urgency**.

> ● **High Impact + High Urgency = P1 (Critical Outage):** The main
> database is down, it is the middle of the workday, and the company is
> actively losing money. All available IT staff must drop what they are
> doing to resolve it.
>
> ● **Low Impact + High Urgency = P2 or P3 (High/Medium):** The CEO’s
> laptop will not connect to Wi-Fi, and they have a major presentation
> in 10 minutes.
>
> ● **Low Impact + Low Urgency = P4 (Low):** A user wants a dual-monitor
> setup installed at their desk by next week.
>
> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Do not confuse severity with priority. Severity
> describes the technical damage, but it does NOT directly calculate
> priority. Remember: **I+U=P (Impact + Urgency = Priority).** Priority
> = Impact + Urgency ***only***. Severity is the distractor.

<img src="media/image34.png" style="width:6.49843in;height:7.40171in"
alt="A three-part infographic titled Ticket Prioritization. The top section defines the metrics Severity, Impact, and Urgency. " />

## Quick Review — Operational Procedures

### Questions

1.  A technician receives two tickets simultaneously: the CEO's laptop
    won't connect to Wi-Fi before a presentation in 10 minutes, and a
    warehouse employee wants a second monitor by next week. Using the
    Impact + Urgency = Priority formula, what priority level would each
    receive?

2.  A user reports “the printer is broken.” What should the technician
    ask first to identify the problem?

3.  An angry user says, “This computer is garbage! Nothing works!” Which
    response is most appropriate?

4.  What is the correct order of the 8-step ticket lifecycle?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  CEO ticket: Low Impact (one user) + High Urgency (time-sensitive) =
    P2/P3 (High/Medium priority). Warehouse ticket: Low Impact (one
    user) + Low Urgency (no time pressure) = P4 (Low priority). Handle
    the CEO ticket first.

2.  “What were you doing when the problem started?” or “Can you describe
    exactly what happens when you try to print?” — open-ended questions
    that gather symptoms without leading the user or making assumptions.

3.  “I understand this is frustrating. I'm here to help.” — Acknowledge
    the user's frustration without being defensive, then offer a
    collaborative solution. Never say “calm down” or blame the user.

4.  Ticket Creation → Categorization & Prioritization → Troubleshooting
    & Documentation → Escalation (if needed) → Resolution &
    Implementation → Verification → User Education → Ticket Closure
    (closure is ALWAYS last, AFTER user confirms resolution).

# Module: Software Troubleshooting

## Module Objectives

Software troubleshooting is 23% of the Core 2 exam - one of the largest
domains. Technicians must diagnose and resolve issues across operating
systems, applications, browsers, and mobile platforms.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Diagnose and resolve common Windows OS errors and performance
> issues.
>
> ● Troubleshoot application installation, update, and compatibility
> problems.
>
> ● Identify and resolve browser and internet application issues.
>
> ● Troubleshoot common mobile OS problems on iOS and Android.
>
> ● Address security-related software symptoms (malware, unauthorized
> access).
>
> ● Use appropriate tools to diagnose software-level failures.

## Windows OS Troubleshooting

### Common Windows Error Screens & Startup Problems

**Blue Screen of Death (BSOD)**\
Likely Cause: Driver failure, RAM error, corrupted system files,
hardware fault.\
Recommended Fix: Note the error code. Run memory diagnostic. Check Event
Viewer for details. Roll back recent drivers.

**“No Boot Device Found” / “No Operating System”**\
Likely Cause: Corrupted bootloader, missing boot partition, failed
drive.\
Recommended Fix: Boot to Safe Mode. Run sfc /scannow. Check Windows
Update history. Repair or roll back update.

**System stuck at login screen / spinning circle**\
Likely Cause: Corrupted Windows Update, profile corruption.\
Recommended Fix: Boot to Safe Mode. Run sfc /scannow. Check Windows
Update history. Repair or roll back update.

**Slow startup**\
Likely Cause: Too many startup programs, fragmented HDD, low RAM.\
Recommended Fix: Task Manager → Startup tab. Run Disk Cleanup. Upgrade
to SSD. Check for malware.

**System randomly reboots / shuts down**\
Likely Cause: Overheating, failing RAM, power supply issue.\
Recommended Fix: Check CPU temps. Run Windows Memory Diagnostic. Inspect
PSU and fans.

**Black screen after login**\
Likely Cause: Corrupted display driver, Remote Desktop conflict,
explorer.exe crash.\
Recommended Fix: Use Ctrl+Alt+Del. Open Task Manager. Run new task:
explorer.exe. Update/roll back display driver.

### Windows Performance Issues
