**High CPU usage** — Open Task Manager → Processes tab. Identify the
process consuming CPU. Check for malware (high svchost.exe usage is a
common malware sign). Disable unnecessary startup programs.

**High RAM usage** — Task Manager → Performance tab. Identify
memory-hungry processes. Consider adding RAM. Check for memory leaks in
applications.

**High Disk usage (100%)** — Common with HDDs and can cause severe
slowdowns. Disable Superfetch/SysMain in Services. Check for malware.
Consider replacing HDD with SSD.

**App not responding (frozen)** — Task Manager → right-click → End Task.
If recurring, update or reinstall the application. Check Event Viewer
for crash logs.

## Application Troubleshooting

### Installation & Update Problems

**Application fails to install**\
Likely Cause: Insufficient disk space, missing dependencies, corrupt
installer, permission issues.\
Fix: Free disk space. Run installer as administrator. Download fresh
installer. Check Windows Event Log.

**Application won’t update**\
Likely Cause: Corrupted update cache, network issue, insufficient
permissions.\
Fix: Clear application cache/temp files. Re-run update as admin.
Uninstall and reinstall.

**Application won’t start / immediately crashes**\
Likely Cause: Corrupt app files, missing Visual C++ or .NET runtime,
incompatible OS version.\
Fix: Reinstall the application. Install required runtimes. Check
compatibility settings (right-click → Properties → Compatibility).

**Error: “DLL not found” / “Missing file”**\
Likely Cause: Missing or corrupted dependency files.\
Fix: Reinstall the application. Run sfc /scannow. Reinstall Visual C++
Redistributable or .NET Framework.

**“Access Denied” during installation**\
Likely Cause: UAC restrictions, insufficient permissions.\
Fix: Right-click installer → “Run as administrator.” Verify user account
has admin rights.

### Application Compatibility

When an application was designed for an older version of Windows and
fails to run correctly:

> ● Right-click the application → Properties → Compatibility tab.
>
> ● Enable “Run this program in compatibility mode for:” and select the
> appropriate OS version.
>
> ● Enable “Run this program as an administrator” if needed.

## Browser & Internet Troubleshooting

When a user submits a ticket regarding browser issues, a technician must
quickly distinguish between a simple misconfiguration, a local privacy
misunderstanding, and an active attack. The table below maps common
symptoms to their root cause concepts and the correct fix.

**Browser won't load pages (internet works)**\
*Root Cause & Concept*: Cache/Cookie Corruption or Proxy
Misconfiguration. The browser has stale or corrupted local data, or
traffic is being routed through a broken proxy.\
*Technician's Fix*: Clear cache and cookies. Check proxy settings. Flush
DNS (ipconfig /flushdns). Test in a different browser to isolate the
issue.

**Specific sites won't load**\
*Root Cause & Concept*: DNS Resolution Failure or Hosts File Tampering.
Malware commonly modifies the hosts file to redirect or block specific
domains. Browser extensions can also interfere.\
*Technician's Fix*: Check the hosts file
(%SystemRoot%\System32\drivers\etc\hosts) for unauthorized entries. Try
incognito mode. Disable extensions. Verify DNS settings.

**Slow browser performance**\
*Root Cause & Concept*: Extension Bloat, Excessive Tabs, or Malware. Too
many active extensions consume memory; malware may run background
processes through the browser.\
*Technician's Fix*: Disable unnecessary extensions. Update the browser.
Run a malware scan.

**“Your connection is not private” on every HTTPS site**\
*Root Cause & Concep*t: Incorrect System Clock. SSL/TLS certificates
rely on strict time validation. A clock set years in the past causes
every modern certificate to appear mathematically invalid.\
*Technician's Fix*: Verify and sync the OS date and time settings with
an NTP server. This resolves the majority of widespread certificate
errors immediately.

**Certificate name mismatch warning**\
*Root Cause & Concept*: DNS Hijacking or On-Path Attack. The browser
requested bank.com but the certificate returned identifies a different
server — a serious red flag.\
*Technician's Fix*: Do not bypass this warning. Flush the local DNS
cache (ipconfig /flushdns). Check the router for unauthorized DNS server
entries.

**SSL/TLS certificate error — expired or untrusted CA**\
*Root Cause & Concept*: Expired Certificate or Self-Signed Certificate.
The site's certificate has lapsed, or it was issued by a CA not trusted
by the browser (common on internal corporate tools).\
*Technician's Fix*: Warn the user — do not dismiss on sensitive sites.
For internal tools, the IT team may need to install the organization's
root CA certificate.

**Browser homepage or search engine changed**\
*Root Cause & Concept*: Browser Hijacker. Malicious code has injected
itself into the browser's core configuration.\
*Technician's Fix*: Run a full anti-malware scan. Perform a complete
browser reset via browser settings to wipe all malicious modifications.
Remove suspicious extensions.

**Pop-up ads and redirects**\
*Root Cause & Concept*: Adware or Browser Hijacker Infection. Same root
cause as homepage change — often installed as a bundle with freeware.\
*Technician's Fix*: Remove suspicious extensions. Run an anti-malware
scan. Reset browser settings to default.

**Extension requesting unrelated permissions (e.g., a flashlight app
asking for “access to all website data”)**\
*Root Cause & Concept*: Over-Privileged Extension. Malicious developers
use simple utility tools as Trojan horses to harvest user data across
every site visited.\
*Technician's Fix*: Enforce the principle of least privilege. Remove any
extension requesting permissions unrelated to its stated function. Only
install extensions from official stores.

> **✔ Pro Tip:** Wrong system clock makes every SSL certificate appear
> invalid. Always check date/time **first** before diving deeper into
> certificate errors. - Private/Incognito mode only prevents **local**
> history from being saved - it does not hide traffic from ISPs,
> employers, or network monitoring tools. - Homepage change +
> redirects + pop-up ads = browser hijacker. The fix is always: malware
> scan + browser reset. - A **certificate name mismatch** is not a
> routine error - it can indicate DNS hijacking. Treat it seriously,
> especially on financial or authentication sites. - **Hosts file
> tampering** is a classic malware technique for blocking security sites
> or redirecting banking domains.

## Mobile OS Troubleshooting

### Common Mobile Software Issues

**App crashes immediately**\
Likely Cause: Corrupt app data, incompatible OS version, insufficient
storage.\
Fix: Clear app cache (Android: Settings → Apps → Storage). Update or
reinstall app. Free storage space.

**Device running slowly / freezing**\
Likely Cause: Too many background apps, low storage, outdated OS.\
Fix: Close background apps. Restart device. Free storage (delete unused
apps/photos). Update OS.

**Wi-Fi connects but no internet**\
Likely Cause: IP conflict, DNS issue, router-side problem.\
Fix: Forget the network and reconnect. Toggle Airplane Mode. Check if
other devices can connect.

**Mobile data not working**\
Likely Cause: APN settings incorrect, SIM card issue, data limit
reached.\
Fix: Check APN settings (carrier-specific). Reinsert SIM. Check data
plan usage.

**Device won’t charge**\
Likely Cause: Dirty/damaged charging port, faulty cable/adapter, battery
issue.\
Fix: Try different cable/adapter. Clean port with compressed air.
Restart device.

**Location services inaccurate**\
Likely Cause: GPS off, poor signal, app permissions.\
Fix: Enable High Accuracy mode. Ensure app has location permission.
Toggle GPS off/on.

**Touch screen unresponsive**\
Likely Cause: Screen protector interference, software glitch, digitizer
failure.\
Fix: Restart device. Remove screen protector. Test with different input.
May require hardware repair.

**Cannot receive calls or texts**\
Likely Cause: Airplane mode enabled, SIM issue, carrier outage.\
Fix: Disable Airplane Mode. Reinsert SIM. Check carrier network status.

### Mobile OS Reset Options

When software issues cannot be resolved through normal troubleshooting,
a reset may be required:

**Soft Reset** — Restarting or rebooting the device. Resolves minor
glitches, freezes, and performance issues. No data loss.

**Network Settings Reset** — Resets all Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, cellular, and
VPN settings to defaults. Use when network connectivity cannot be
restored by normal means.

**Factory Reset / Hard Reset** — Erases all data and returns the device
to factory settings. Use as a last resort for unfixable software issues
or before selling/reassigning a device. ALWAYS back up data first.

## Security-Related Software Symptoms

Some software issues are symptoms of a security compromise rather than a
hardware or software defect. Technicians must recognize these patterns:

**Antivirus suddenly disabled or uninstalled** — Malware often disables
AV to prevent detection. Boot from external media or Safe Mode to run AV
scan.

**New, unexpected user accounts appear** — Possible backdoor or
unauthorized account creation. Review accounts in Control Panel /
Settings. Remove unknown accounts.

**System files missing or altered** — Malware, rootkit, or ransomware.
Run sfc /scannow. Use anti-rootkit tools. Consider OS reinstall.

**Pop-up warning saying “Your PC is infected – call this number”** —
Tech support scam (scareware). Do NOT call the number. Close browser via
Task Manager if needed. Run legitimate AV scan.

**Files encrypted with ransom message** — Ransomware infection. Do NOT
pay ransom. Disconnect from network immediately. Restore from backup.
Report to management.

**Slow performance with high disk/network activity (no user action)** —
Possible malware, cryptomining, or botnet activity. Check Task Manager
and netstat. Run AV/malware scan.

## Software Troubleshooting Tools

**Task Manager** — Monitor CPU/RAM/Disk/Network usage. Kill unresponsive
processes. Manage startup programs.

**Event Viewer** — View application, system, and security logs for error
codes and crash details.

**Device Manager** — Check for driver errors (yellow exclamation marks).
Roll back or update drivers.

**Reliability Monitor** — Windows tool (in Control Panel) showing a
visual timeline of system stability and recent failures.

**Resource Monitor** — Detailed view of CPU, memory, disk, and network
usage per process.

**sfc /scannow** — Scans and repairs corrupted Windows system files.

**DISM /Online /Cleanup-Image /RestoreHealth** — Repairs the Windows
image used by sfc. Run before sfc if system image is corrupted.

**System Restore** — Returns system settings to a previous restore point
without affecting personal files.

**msconfig (System Configuration)** — Manage startup programs and
services. Enable Selective or Diagnostic Startup to isolate issues.

**Safe Mode** — Boots Windows with only essential drivers. Used to
diagnose whether the issue is caused by drivers or startup programs.

# Module: Hardware Troubleshooting

## Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Recognize common hardware failure symptoms.
>
> ● Diagnose and troubleshoot issues with desktops, laptops, components,
> and peripherals.
>
> ● Understand POST codes, BIOS/UEFI indicators, and error messages.
>
> ● Apply the CompTIA troubleshooting model systematically.

## Common Hardware Symptoms

**No Power / System Won’t Turn On**\
Symptoms & Likely Causes: No fans, no lights, no display, no beeps.
Causes: Failed PSU, bad power cable, motherboard short.\
Fix: Test with known-good PSU. Reseat power connectors (ATX 24-pin, CPU
8-pin). Check surge protector or outlet.

**POST Errors & Beep Codes**\
Symptoms & Likely Causes: Causes: Bad RAM, missing CPU fan, no GPU
detected, motherboard fault.\
Fix: Reseat/replace RAM modules. Ensure CPU fan is connected.
Install/reseat GPU. Consult motherboard manual for beep patterns.

**No Display / Black Screen**\
Symptoms & Likely Causes: Fans spin, lights on, no video output. Causes:
Loose cable, GPU failure, RAM not seated, BIOS corruption.\
Fix: Test with known-good monitor & cable. Reseat GPU and RAM. Clear
CMOS (remove coin-cell battery 10-15 seconds).

**Overheating**\
Symptoms & Likely Causes: Sudden shutdowns, high fan speeds, thermal
throttling. Causes: Dust buildup, failed fan, dried thermal paste.\
Fix: Remove dust with compressed air. Replace thermal paste (clean old
paste first). Improve airflow.

**RAM Failures**\
Symptoms & Likely Causes: Random reboots, blue screens, application
crashes, failure to POST.\
Fix: Test sticks individually. Run Windows Memory Diagnostic. Clean RAM
contacts. Replace failing module.

**Storage Failures**\
Symptoms & Likely Causes: Slow performance, clicking sounds (HDD), boot
device not found, file corruption.\
Fix: Back up immediately. Replace failing drive. Check SSD firmware.
Verify SATA/power cables.

**Low IOPS\**
Symptoms & Likely Causes: Sluggish multi-user or database performance
even though raw throughput (MB/s) looks normal. Causes: drive can't keep
up with many small simultaneous read/write requests.\
Fix: Check IOPS rating vs. workload demand. Consider SSD/NVMe upgrade or
RAID striping to increase available IOPS.

**PSU Failures**\
Symptoms & Likely Causes: Burning smell, intermittent shutdowns, random
reboots, PSU fan not spinning.\
Fix: Replace PSU (no internal repair). Ensure wattage is sufficient for
CPU/GPU.

## Warning Signs of Hardware Failure

**Burning smell** — PSU or motherboard failure.

**Clicking sounds** — HDD head crash.

**Bulging capacitors** — Motherboard or PSU issue.

**Frequent BSODs** — RAM or storage failure.

**Artifacting on screen** — GPU overheating/failure.

**System clock resets** — Dead CMOS battery.

# Module: Networking Troubleshooting

## Module Objectives

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Diagnose wired, wireless, and internet connectivity issues.
>
> ● Recognize symptoms such as APIPA addresses, DNS failures, slow
> speeds, and IP conflicts.
>
> ● Use network diagnostic tools (software + hardware).
>
> ● Support SOHO (Small Office/Home Office) networking environments.

## Common Network Symptoms

**No Connectivity**\
Likely Causes: “No Internet” message, Red X on network icon, cannot ping
gateway. Causes: Unplugged cable, disabled adapter, bad port, router
offline.\
Fix: Check cable connection. Reset router. Enable adapter in OS. Test
another port.

**Limited or Intermittent Connectivity**\
Likely Causes: Frequent disconnects, slow loading, Wi-Fi icon flickers.
Causes: Weak signal, interference, congested channel, DHCP lease
expiring.\
Fix: Move closer to AP. Change Wi-Fi channel. Restart router. Renew IP
address.

**Port Flapping**\
Likely Causes: A switch port repeatedly transitions up/down. Causes:
failing cable, faulty NIC, duplex mismatch, loose connection.\
Fix: Check switch logs. Replace cable. Test port with known-good device.
Verify duplex/speed settings match on both ends.

**APIPA Address (169.254.x.x)**\
Likely Causes: Device did not receive DHCP IP - OS assigns a fallback
self-assigned address. Causes: DHCP server offline, DHCP scope full, bad
cable.\
Fix: Restart DHCP server/router. Run ipconfig /release + ipconfig
/renew. Reconnect to Wi-Fi with correct password.

**DNS Issues (Can’t Resolve Names)**\
Likely Causes: Can ping IP but not hostname. “DNS server not found.”
Causes: DNS server down, incorrect settings, malware modifying DNS.\
Fix: Flush DNS (ipconfig /flushdns). Set DNS to automatic or use public
DNS (8.8.8.8). Check hosts file. Run malware scan.

**Slow Performance on Network**\
Likely Causes: Long loading, video buffering, high ping. Causes:
Bandwidth saturation, Wi-Fi interference, faulty cables, ISP
congestion.\
Fix: Check for heavy downloads. Replace cable (Cat6 preferred). Move
device/AP. Reset modem.

**IP Address Conflicts**\
Likely Causes: “IP address already in use,” random disconnects. Causes:
Static IP duplicates DHCP address.\
Fix: Change static IP. Use DHCP. Reserve IPs correctly in DHCP scope.

## Diagnostic &Troubleshooting Tools

### Windows Command-Line Tools

**ipconfig** — Shows IP info; renew DHCP leases.

**ping** — Tests connectivity to a host.

**tracert** — Traces route to destination.

**netstat** — Shows active network connections.

**nslookup** — Tests DNS name resolution.

**ipconfig /flushdns** — Clears the local DNS cache.

### Essential Hardware Troubleshooting Tools

**Multimeter** — Test voltage, continuity, power rails.

**Power Supply Tester** — Quickly checks PSU health.

**POST Card** — Diagnoses motherboard POST errors.

**Loopback Plug** — Tests NIC transmit/receive functions.

**Compressed Air** — Removes dust blocking cooling systems.

**Anti-static Wrist Strap & Mat** — Prevents ESD damage.

**Cable Tester** — Checks continuity & wiring issues.

**Tone Generator & Probe** — Identifies cable runs in walls.

**Crimper** — Installs RJ-45 ends on UTP cables

**Punch-down Tool** — Terminates cables on patch panels.

**Precision Screwdriver Set** — Provides access to micro screws to use
on modern PC components and cases.

<img src="media/image35.png" style="width:6.5in;height:5.76177in"
alt="An 11-panel reference grid titled &#39;Essential Hardware Troubleshooting Tools.&#39; " />

## Quick Review — Domain Troubleshooting

### Questions

1.  A Windows system won't boot after a driver installation. Using
    WinRE, what is the first recovery approach?

2.  A device receives a 169.254.x.x IP address. What does this indicate,
    and what command should the technician run to attempt resolution?

3.  A user reports their laptop “runs slow.” What should the technician
    check first in Task Manager?

4.  A legacy application won't run on Windows 11. What is the first
    thing a technician should try before escalating?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  Boot into Safe Mode and roll back the driver via Device Manager. If
    that fails, use System Restore to return to a point before the
    driver was installed.

2.  It indicates the device cannot reach a DHCP server (APIPA fallback
    address). Run \`ipconfig /release\` followed by \`ipconfig /renew\`
    to force a new DHCP request.

3.  Check the Processes tab to identify which process is consuming
    excessive CPU, RAM, or disk resources. High disk usage (100%) is a
    common cause of perceived slowness.

4.  Right-click the application → Properties → Compatibility tab →
    enable “Run this program in compatibility mode” and select the
    appropriate older Windows version. This resolves most legacy app
    issues.

# Module: Scripting Overview

## Module Objectives

Scripting automates repetitive or complex tasks. Unlike compiled
programs, scripts are interpreted line-by-line, making them lightweight,
flexible, and ideal for system administration.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Identify and describe common scripting languages used in IT
> environments.
>
> ● Understand essential scripting concepts including variables, loops,
> conditionals, and functions.
>
> ● Troubleshoot common scripting errors including syntax issues,
> permissions, and missing modules.

## Common Scripting Languages

**PowerShell**\
Purpose: Windows automation and administrative tasks.\
File Type: .ps1

**Bash**\
Purpose: Linux/macOS system and file operations.\
File Type: .sh

**Python**\
Purpose: Cross-platform automation, parsing, and tooling.\
File Type: .py

**JavaScript**\
Purpose: Browser automation and UI manipulation.\
File Type: .js

**Visual Basic Script**\
Purpose: Legacy Windows automation.\
File Type: .vbs

## Core Scripting Concepts

**Variables** — Store data for use in scripts.

**Conditionals** — Allow scripts to perform different actions depending
on the situation.

**Loops** — Repeat tasks automatically, ideal for system scans or file
operations.

**Functions** — Reusable code blocks.

**Arguments** — Parameters for flexible input.

**Comments** — For documentation - exclude line from code (# // \<#
\#\>).

**Shebang** — Defines the interpreter at the top of a script (e.g.,
\#!/bin/bash).

## Security Considerations

Key principles when working with scripts:

> ● PowerShell Execution Policies: Restricted (no scripts run),
> RemoteSigned (local scripts run; downloaded must be signed),
> Unrestricted (not recommended).
>
> ● Linux/macOS Permissions: Scripts must be made executable: chmod +x
> script.sh
>
> ● Only run scripts from trusted sources. Avoid executing scripts
> delivered via email or chat.
>
> ● Follow Least Privilege: Run scripts with minimum necessary rights.
> Only elevate when required.
>
> ● Avoid Destructive Commands: Be cautious with rm -rf / (Linux) or del
> /s /q C: (Windows).

## Common Scripting Errors

**Syntax Error**\
Symptom: Script fails immediately with a parsing or unexpected token
error.\
Fix: Review the flagged line. Check for missing quotes, brackets, or
incorrect operators. Run python -m py_compile script.py or pwsh -Command
“Get-Command” to validate.

**Permission Error**\
Symptom: “Access denied,” “permission denied,” or script won’t execute
at all.\
Fix: Windows: Run PowerShell as administrator. Check execution policy:
run Get-ExecutionPolicy. Set with Set-ExecutionPolicy RemoteSigned.
Linux/macOS: Run chmod +x script.sh to make the file executable. Use
sudo if elevated rights are needed.

**Missing Module / Not Found**\
Symptom: “Module not found,” “cannot find module,” or “command not
recognized.”\
Fix: Python: Install the missing module with pip install
\<module-name\>. PowerShell: Install with Install-Module
\<module-name\>. Bash: Install missing tools via apt-get install or yum
install.

**Wrong Interpreter**\
Symptom: Script runs but produces unexpected output or fails with an
unrecognized command error.\
Fix: Verify the shebang line (#!/bin/bash, \#!/usr/bin/env python3).
Confirm the correct interpreter is installed and in the system PATH.

**Path Error**\
Symptom: “File not found” even though the file exists.\
Fix: Use absolute paths instead of relative paths. Verify the working
directory with pwd (Linux) or cd (Windows). Check for typos in the path.

Use the quick-reference chart to match an error message to its fix at a
glance — the table above walks through the “why,” this walks through
rapid pattern recognition for troubleshooting on the fly.

<img src="media/image36.png" style="width:6.51042in;height:7.24306in"
alt="Common scripting errors guide, matching error messages to fixes for Syntax, Permission, Module, Interpreter, and Path-related issues." />

# Module: Data Destruction & Disposal

## Module Objectives

Before disposing of, reselling, or repurposing any device, IT
technicians must ensure that sensitive data is completely and
unrecoverably destroyed. Improper disposal is one of the most common
sources of data breaches.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Identify the correct data destruction method for different media
> types.
>
> ● Differentiate between physical and software-based destruction
> methods.
>
> ● Understand regulatory and compliance requirements for data disposal.
>
> ● Explain what a Certificate of Destruction is and when it is
> required.

## Why Data Destruction Matters

Simply deleting files or even formatting a drive does NOT permanently
erase data. Standard deletion removes only the file pointer - the actual
data remains on the storage medium and can be recovered with forensic
tools. Proper destruction ensures data cannot be recovered by any means.

## Physical Destruction Methods

Physical destruction is the most thorough and irreversible method. Used
when the device will not be reused.

**Shredding** — Industrial shredders physically destroy hard drives,
SSDs, optical discs, and documents into tiny pieces. The gold standard
for complete destruction. Often used with a Certificate of Destruction.

**Drilling / Punching** — Drilling holes through a hard drive platter or
SSD destroys the storage media. Quick and effective for individual
drives. Pieces may still be recoverable with advanced forensics.

**Degaussing** — Exposes magnetic storage (HDDs, magnetic tape) to a
powerful magnetic field that scrambles all data. Effective for HDDs and
tape but has NO effect on SSDs, flash memory, or optical discs (they
have no magnetic media).

**Incineration** — Complete burning of media in a controlled furnace.
Used for highly sensitive government/military data. Requires certified
destruction facility.

**Pulverizing** — Crushing media into fine dust using industrial
equipment. Similar to shredding - used for high-security environments.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Degaussing only works on magnetic media (HDDs,
> magnetic tape). It has absolutely NO effect on SSDs, USB drives,
> optical discs, or any flash-based storage.

### Software-Based Destruction Methods

Software methods overwrite data on the drive, making it unrecoverable
without physically destroying the media. Used when the device will be
reused, donated, or resold.

**Zero Fill (Single-Pass Overwrite)** — Overwrites every bit on the
drive with zeros. Fast and effective for modern drives. Recommended by
NIST SP 800-88 for most situations.

**Multi-Pass Overwrite (DoD 5220.22-M)** — Overwrites data multiple
times (3 or 7 passes) with different patterns. Once considered the gold
standard but modern research shows single-pass is equally effective on
current drives.

**Secure Erase (ATA)** — A built-in firmware command available on most
modern SSDs and HDDs that resets all storage cells to factory state.
Fast and highly effective - recommended for SSDs over multi-pass
overwrite.

**Cryptographic Erasure (Crypto Shredding)** — Encrypts all data on the
drive with a strong key, then destroys the key. The data is still
present but permanently unreadable without the key. Very fast and
effective.

**Factory Reset** — Restores a device to factory settings. For mobile
devices with hardware encryption enabled, this effectively destroys the
encryption key, rendering data unrecoverable. For non-encrypted devices,
data may be partially recoverable.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Multi-pass overwrite (DoD method) is largely
> ineffective on SSDs due to wear-leveling - the drive’s firmware may
> skip sectors. Use Secure Erase or cryptographic erasure for SSDs
> instead.

<img src="media/image37.png" style="width:6.46875in;height:8.48854in"
alt="Data destruction and disposal methods matrix." />

## Certificate of Destruction

A Certificate of Destruction (CoD) is a formal document provided by a
certified destruction vendor confirming that specified media was
destroyed in compliance with relevant regulations. It includes:

> ● Description and serial numbers of the destroyed media.
>
> ● Date and method of destruction.
>
> ● Name and signature of the certified destruction provider.
>
> ● Chain of custody documentation.

Organizations required by regulations (HIPAA, GDPR, PCI-DSS, SOX) to
protect sensitive data must retain Certificates of Destruction as
evidence of compliance.

> **✔ Pro Tip:** Degaussing = magnetic destruction ONLY. For SSDs use
> Secure Erase or physical destruction. Certificate of Destruction is
> required when regulations (HIPAA, PCI-DSS, GDPR) mandate proof of
> disposal.. Deleting files + emptying the recycle bin does NOT destroy
> data.

## Choosing the Right Method

**Reselling a corporate laptop with an HDD**\
Recommended Method: Multi-pass overwrite or Zero Fill\
Why: Drive will be reused — physical destruction not needed. Overwrite
renders data unrecoverable.

**Disposing of an old SSD from an HR workstation**\
Recommended Method: Secure Erase or physical shredding\
Why: Multi-pass overwrite is unreliable on SSDs. Secure Erase or
destruction is preferred.

**Decommissioning a server with magnetic tape backups**\
Recommended Method: Degaussing + shredding\
Why: Degaussing destroys tape data; shredding provides physical
assurance.

**Repurposing a company phone (encrypted)**\
Recommended Method: Factory reset\
Why: Hardware encryption + factory reset destroys the key, making data
unrecoverable.

**Disposing of drives containing classified information**\
Recommended Method: Physical shredding + Certificate of Destruction\
Why: Highest security requirement - physical destruction with documented
chain of custody.

**Retiring dozens of old laptops for charity donation**\
Recommended Method: Zero fill overwrite\
Why: Cost-effective, allows reuse, meets standard compliance
requirements.

# Module: Licensing & Regulated Data

## Module Objectives

IT technicians regularly deal with software licensing compliance and
must understand the types of data their organization handles and the
regulations that govern it. Mishandling either can expose the
organization to significant legal and financial risk.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Identify and describe common software license types.
>
> ● Explain the consequences of non-compliance with licensing terms.
>
> ● Define PII, PHI, PCI, and other regulated data categories.
>
> ● Describe basic requirements under GDPR, HIPAA, and PCI-DSS.
>
> ● Understand the IT technician’s role in data protection compliance.

## Software Licensing Types

**OEM (Original Equipment Manufacturer)** — Software licensed to a
specific piece of hardware. Cannot be transferred to a different device.
Typically pre-installed by the manufacturer. Example: Windows that comes
with a new PC.

**Retail / Full Packaged Product** — Purchased by a consumer and can be
transferred to a new device (by uninstalling from the old one). More
flexible than OEM. Example: Purchasing a boxed copy of software.

**Volume License** — A single license agreement covering multiple
installations across an organization. Managed through a central portal.
Cost-effective for businesses. Example: Microsoft Volume Licensing,
Microsoft 365 Business.

**Subscription** — Recurring payment (monthly/annual) for ongoing access
to software. Stops working when the subscription lapses. Includes
automatic updates. Examples: Microsoft 365, Adobe Creative Cloud.

**Open Source** — Source code is publicly available. Use, modification,
and distribution are governed by specific open-source licenses (GPL,
MIT, Apache). Free but comes with obligations (e.g., GPL requires
sharing modifications).

**Freeware** — Free to use but source code is not provided and
modification/redistribution is restricted. Different from open source.
Example: most free utility tools.

**Shareware** — Try-before-you-buy model. Functional version distributed
freely for a trial period; full features require purchase.

**End-User License Agreement (EULA)** — The legal contract between the
software vendor and the user defining how the software may be used. Must
be accepted during installation. IT must ensure compliance.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** OEM licenses are tied to the hardware - you cannot
> move them to a different machine. Retail licenses can be transferred.
> Volume licenses belong to the organization, not individual users.

## Regulated Data Categories

Certain types of data are subject to legal regulations that impose
specific requirements for how they must be stored, accessed,
transmitted, and disposed of. IT technicians must recognize these
categories.

**PII (Personally Identifiable Information)** — Any data that can
identify a specific individual: name, address, SSN, email, phone, date
of birth, IP address, biometric data. Protected under multiple
regulations including GDPR and many US state laws.

**PHI (Protected Health Information)** — Any health information that can
identify a patient: diagnoses, treatment records, insurance information,
test results. Regulated under HIPAA in the United States.

**PCI DSS Data** — Payment card data: cardholder names, card numbers,
CVVs, expiration dates. Regulated under the Payment Card Industry Data
Security Standard (PCI-DSS).

**Intellectual Property (IP)** — Proprietary business information: trade
secrets, source code, product designs, business strategies. Protected
under copyright, patent, and trade secret law.

**FERPA Data** — Student education records. Protected under the Family
Educational Rights and Privacy Act in the United States. Applies to
schools receiving federal funding.

**Corporate Confidential** — Internal business data not legally
regulated but protected by employment contracts and NDA agreements:
financial projections, acquisition plans, HR data.

## Key Regulations ToKnow

**GDPR**\
(General Data Protection Regulation) — EU regulation governing PII of EU
residents. Requires: explicit consent for data collection, right to
erasure (“right to be forgotten”), data breach notification within 72
hours, data minimization. Applies to any organization serving EU
residents regardless of location.

**HIPAA**\
(Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act) — US law
protecting PHI. Requires: access controls limiting PHI to those who need
it, audit logs of all PHI access, encryption of PHI in transit and at
rest, secure disposal of PHI media, Business Associate Agreements with
vendors.

**PCI-DSS**\
(Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard) — Standard for
organizations handling payment cards. Requires: encrypted transmission
of cardholder data, restricted access to card data, regular security
testing, not storing CVV codes after authorization.

**SOX**\
(Sarbanes-Oxley Act) — US law for public companies requiring accurate
financial reporting and IT controls around financial data systems. IT
must maintain audit trails and access controls for financial systems.

## IT Technician’s Role in Compliance

> ● Implement access controls ensuring only authorized personnel can
> access regulated data.
>
> ● Ensure devices handling regulated data use full-disk encryption.
>
> ● Follow proper data disposal procedures (see Data Destruction module)
> for media containing regulated data.
>
> ● Never share, copy, or access regulated data beyond what is needed
> for the job (principle of least privilege).
>
> ● Report suspected data breaches immediately - most regulations have
> strict breach notification deadlines.
>
> ● Ensure software licenses are current and compliant - unlicensed
> software creates legal liability.

### Consequences of Non-Compliance

Software vendor audits result in forced license purchases and fines.
Legal action and financial penalties. Loss of access (subscription
termination). Organizational reputation damage. Personal liability for
IT staff who knowingly allow unlicensed software.

# Module: Asset Management & Documentation

## Module Objectives

Asset management and documentation are core operational responsibilities
for IT professionals. Proper documentation reduces troubleshooting time,
ensures compliance, and maintains organizational knowledge when staff
changes occur.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Explain the purpose and components of an IT asset management system.
>
> ● Identify and describe common types of IT documentation.
>
> ● Understand the importance of change management documentation.
>
> ● Create and maintain basic network and system documentation.

## Asset Management

IT asset management (ITAM) tracks hardware and software assets
throughout their entire lifecycle - from procurement and deployment
through maintenance and final disposal.

**Asset Tag / Barcode** — A physical label (barcode or QR code) applied
to equipment for inventory tracking. Tied to a database record
containing the device’s full details.

**Asset Inventory Database** — Central repository recording all hardware
and software assets. Includes: make/model, serial number, purchase date,
warranty, assigned user, location, and current status.

**Procurement Records** — Documentation of when and how assets were
purchased, including vendor invoices, purchase orders, and licensing
agreements.

**Warranty & Support Records** — Tracks warranty expiration dates,
support contract terms, and vendor contact information for each asset.

**Disposal Records** — Documents how decommissioned assets were
handled - sold, donated, recycled, or destroyed. Includes Certificates
of Destruction where required.

**Software Licensing** — Tracks software licenses (number of seats,
license type, renewal dates) to ensure compliance and avoid
over/under-licensing.

**CMDB (Configuration Management Database)** — A centralized database
that stores information about IT assets (hardware, software,
configurations) and the relationships between them. Used to track
dependencies and support change management and troubleshooting.

## IT Documentation Types

**Network Diagram** — Visual map of the network infrastructure showing
devices (routers, switches, servers, APs), their connections, IP
addresses, and VLANs. Essential for troubleshooting and planning.

**Network Topology Map** — Shows the physical and logical layout of the
network. Logical topology shows data flow; physical topology shows
actual cable and device placement.

**Standard Operating Procedure (SOP)** — Step-by-step instructions for
routine IT tasks (e.g., onboarding a new user, deploying a workstation,
responding to a security incident). Ensures consistency regardless of
who performs the task.

**Knowledge Base Article** — A searchable document describing how to
resolve a specific issue, configure a system, or understand a
technology. Built from resolved tickets and technician experience.

**Runbook / Playbook** — Detailed procedures for specific operational or
incident response scenarios. Similar to SOPs but often more granular and
scenario-specific.

**Change Log** — A record of all changes made to systems,
configurations, or infrastructure. Includes what changed, when, by whom,
why, and the approval reference.

**Incident Report** — Documents a security incident or major outage from
detection through resolution. Includes timeline, impact, root cause, and
corrective actions taken.

**Acceptable Use Policy (AUP)** — Policy document defining what
employees are and are not allowed to do with company IT resources.

**Disaster Recovery Plan (DRP)** — Documented procedures for restoring
IT systems and operations following a catastrophic event (fire, flood,
ransomware attack, etc.).

**Business Continuity Plan (BCP)** — Broader than a DRP - covers how the
entire business continues operating during and after a disruption, not
just IT systems.

## Change Management

Change management is the formal process for requesting, evaluating,
approving, implementing, and documenting changes to IT systems. It
prevents unplanned outages caused by untested changes.

**1. Documented Business Processes** — The formal reason and business
justification for the change is recorded.

**2. Purpose of the Change** — Clearly defines *why* the change is
needed (new requirement, bug fix, security patch, etc.).

**3. Scope of the Change** — Defines what systems, users, or processes
will be affected.

**4. Risk Analysis** — Evaluates potential risks and assigns a risk
level (low/medium/high).

**5. Plan for the Change** — Documents exactly how the change will be
implemented, including a timeline.

**6. End-User Acceptance** — Confirms affected users understand and
accept the upcoming change.

**7. Change Board Approval** — The CAB reviews and formally approves the
change request.

**8. Backout Plan** — Documents exactly how to revert the change if
something goes wrong.

**9. Document the Changes** — The change log is updated with the full
record after implementation.

## Additional Change Management Terms

**Change Freeze** — A defined period during which no non-emergency
changes are permitted (e.g., a company's busiest sales season), reducing
risk of disruption during critical business windows.

**Peer Review** — Having another qualified technician review a planned
change before implementation, to catch errors or overlooked risks the
original author may have missed.

**Standard Change** — A low-risk, pre-approved, repeatable change (e.g.,
routine patching) that doesn't require full CAB review each time.

**Normal Change** — A change that follows the full change management
process, including CAB review, due to higher risk or complexity.

**Emergency Change** — A change implemented urgently to resolve a
critical issue, often bypassing the normal approval workflow, with
formal documentation completed after the fact.

<img src="media/image38.png" style="width:6.5in;height:4.21528in"
alt="Flowchart illustrating a 9-step change management process for requesting, approving." />

> **✔ Pro Tip:** Network diagrams and documentation save enormous time
> during troubleshooting - especially in complex environments. - Change
> management prevents “it worked fine before” problems - always document
> changes. - Knowledge base articles are built from closed help desk
> tickets - good ticket documentation builds the KB. - Asset tags
> connect physical hardware to the inventory database - serial number +
> asset tag = full device history.

# Module: Artificial Intelligence (AI) Basics

## Module Objectives

Artificial Intelligence is a new addition to the CompTIA A+ V15 exam
objectives. IT technicians are expected to understand basic AI concepts,
how AI tools integrate into business workflows, the limitations of AI,
and the security and ethical considerations that come with AI use in the
workplace.

By the end of this module, learners will be able to:

> ● Define key AI concepts including machine learning, generative AI,
> and large language models.
>
> ● Differentiate between public AI models and private/organizational AI
> models.
>
> ● Identify appropriate and inappropriate use cases for AI tools in an
> IT environment.
>
> ● Recognize the limitations of AI including bias, hallucinations, and
> accuracy concerns.
>
> ● Understand basic AI security and privacy considerations.

## Key AI Concepts

**Artificial Intelligence (AI)** — The simulation of human intelligence
in machines - enabling systems to perform tasks that typically require
human thinking (learning, reasoning, problem-solving).

**Machine Learning (ML)** — A subset of AI where systems learn from data
to improve performance without being explicitly programmed. The more
data, the better the model learns.

**Generative AI** — AI that creates new content (text, images, code,
audio) based on patterns learned from training data. Examples: ChatGPT,
GitHub Copilot, DALL-E.

**Large Language Model (LLM)** — A type of generative AI trained on
massive text datasets to understand and generate human-like text. Powers
most modern AI chatbots and assistants.

**Natural Language Processing (NLP)** — AI ability to understand,
interpret, and generate human language. Enables voice assistants,
chatbots, and automated translation.

**Neural Network** — A computing system loosely inspired by the human
brain. Layers of interconnected nodes process data to recognize patterns
and make decisions.

**Training Data** — The dataset used to teach an AI model. The quality
and diversity of training data directly affects model accuracy and bias.

## Public vs. Private AI Models

A critical distinction for IT professionals is understanding the
difference between publicly accessible AI tools and private
organizational AI deployments - particularly from a data security
perspective.

**Accessible to anyone via the internet (e.g., ChatGPT, Claude,
Gemini).** — Deployed internally by an organization, not accessible
outside the company.

**Data entered may be used to train future models.** — Data stays within
the organization’s controlled environment.

**Lower cost - often free or subscription-based.** — Higher cost -
requires infrastructure and maintenance.

**Risk: Employees may accidentally share confidential data.** — Designed
to protect sensitive business and customer data.

**Examples: ChatGPT (OpenAI), Copilot (Microsoft), Gemini (Google).** —
Examples: Azure OpenAI Service (private deployment), on-premises LLMs.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Entering sensitive company data, customer PII, or
> trade secrets into a public AI tool is a security violation.
> Organizations must have clear AI use policies that employees follow.

## Appropriate Use Cases for AI in IT

> ● Drafting documentation, knowledge base articles, and SOPs.
>
> ● Summarizing long technical documents or logs.
>
> ● Writing and reviewing code or scripts (with human review).
>
> ● Generating test cases and troubleshooting checklists.
>
> ● Answering general technical questions and providing guidance.
>
> ● Automating repetitive tasks like ticket categorization and report
> generation.

## Inappropriate / Risky Use Cases

> ● Entering customer PII, passwords, or financial data into a public AI
> tool.
>
> ● Using AI-generated code in production without thorough human review.
>
> ● Relying on AI for compliance decisions without expert verification.
>
> ● Sharing internal network diagrams, system configurations, or
> security policies.
>
> ● Using AI output as a final answer for medical, legal, or
> safety-critical decisions.

## AI Limitations To Understand

**Hallucinations** — AI confidently generates information that is
factually incorrect or completely fabricated. Always verify AI output,
especially for technical facts, port numbers, or procedures.

**Training Data Cutoff** — AI models are trained on data up to a certain
date and have no knowledge of events or products released after that
point.

**Bias** — AI can reflect biases present in its training data - leading
to unfair, inaccurate, or skewed outputs, especially in areas involving
people or demographics.

**Context Limitations** — AI models can only process a limited amount of
text at once (context window). Very long documents may be summarized
inaccurately if they exceed this limit.

**No Real-World Verification** — AI cannot independently verify claims
against live systems, databases, or the internet (unless given tools to
do so).

**Privacy Risk** — Data entered into public AI tools may be retained and
used for training. Sensitive data should never be entered into public AI
systems.

## AI Security Considerations

> ● Prompt Injection: Attackers craft malicious inputs designed to
> override an AI’s instructions and make it behave unexpectedly. A
> significant security concern for AI-integrated applications.
>
> ● Data Leakage: Public AI tools may expose confidential data shared in
> prompts. Use private/organizational AI deployments for sensitive
> workflows.
>
> ● Model Poisoning: In ML pipelines, attackers can corrupt training
> data to cause a model to make incorrect decisions. A concern for
> organizations training their own models.
>
> ● Deepfakes: AI-generated realistic fake audio/video of real people.
> Used in social engineering attacks (e.g., fake CEO video call
> requesting a wire transfer).
>
> ● Plagiarism — A key AI policy concern where AI-generated content is
> presented as original human work without disclosure. Organizations
> should have clear policies on when and how AI-assisted content must be
> disclosed or attributed.

## Organizational AI Policies

IT technicians must understand and enforce their organization’s AI
acceptable use policy. Key elements typically include:

> ● Which AI tools are approved for use (whitelist of approved
> platforms).
>
> ● What types of data may and may not be entered into AI systems.
>
> ● Requirements for human review of AI-generated content before use.
>
> ● Reporting procedures for AI-related security incidents.
>
> ● Copyright and intellectual property considerations for AI-generated
> work.
>
> **✔ Pro Tip:** Know the difference between public AI
> (internet-accessible, data privacy risk) and private AI
> (organizational control, data stays internal). - Hallucination = AI
> confidently making things up. Always verify AI output. - AI cannot
> replace human judgment for compliance, legal, medical, or safety
> decisions. - Entering sensitive data into a public AI tool = potential
> security incident. Know your org’s AI use policy.

## Quick Review — Compliance & IT Responsibilities

### Questions

1.  A technician needs to securely erase an SSD containing sensitive
    employee data. The drive will be reused. Why is multi-pass overwrite
    NOT appropriate, and what method should be used instead?

2.  What is the difference between an OEM license and a Retail license
    in terms of transferability?

3.  What is the purpose of a Change Advisory Board (CAB) in the change
    management process?

4.  An employee enters customer credit card numbers into a public AI
    chatbot for analysis. What is the primary security and compliance
    concern?

5.  What must accompany destroyed media containing regulated data (PHI,
    PCI data) to prove compliance?

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Answer Key

1.  Multi-pass overwrite is unreliable on SSDs because wear-leveling
    causes the firmware to skip sectors, leaving data intact. Use the
    Secure Erase (ATA firmware command) instead, which resets all
    storage cells at the firmware level. Alternatively, use
    cryptographic erasure if the drive was encrypted.

2.  OEM licenses are tied to the original hardware and cannot be
    transferred to a different machine. Retail licenses can be
    transferred to a new device by uninstalling from the old one.

3.  The CAB is a group of stakeholders that reviews, evaluates risk, and
    approves or rejects change requests before implementation. It
    prevents untested changes from causing unplanned outages.

4.  Data entered into public AI tools may be retained and used for model
    training, violating PCI-DSS requirements that prohibit sharing
    cardholder data with unauthorized third parties. This constitutes a
    potential data breach and compliance violation. Always use
    private/organizational AI for sensitive data.

5.  \* A Certificate of Destruction — formal documentation from a
    certified destruction vendor confirming that specified media was
    destroyed in compliance with relevant regulations. Required for
    HIPAA, PCI-DSS, GDPR, and other regulated data types.

# EXAM PREPARATION & STUDY TOOLS

This section brings together everything you need for final exam
preparation: mnemonics for every domain, exam traps, quick reference
cards and diagrams, a practice test covering all modules, a 8-week study
plan, and 500 term Glossary.

## Mnemonics and Reference Cards

### Email Protocols

**Memory hook:** *“**Send It, Pop It, Map It**” - SMTP sends out, POP3
pops down to one device, IMAP maps across all devices*

**25 / 587**\
Protocol: SMTP\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: Sends outbound email. Port 25 = server-to-server. Port 587
= client submission with STARTTLS. Always use 587 for client config.

**110 / 995**\
Protocol: POP3 / POP3S\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: Downloads email to one device and removes from server. Port
110 = plain. Port 995 = encrypted (SSL/TLS). Not recommended for
multi-device users.

**143 / 993**\
Protocol: IMAP / IMAPS\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: Keeps email on server and syncs across all devices. Port
143 = plain. Port 993 = encrypted (SSL/TLS). Recommended for modern use.

Quirky Memory Hooks:

> ● **“S**end **M**ore **T**asty **P**izza”(**SMTP** = Sends outbound)
>
> ● **POP3** is like popping a balloon - it’s **one and done**
> (downloads to one device and destroys the server copy).
>
> ● **IMAP** is like a **map** on your phone - no matter where you
> travel or what device you look at, the view stays completely
> synchronized.

### The 6-Step CompTIA Troubleshooting Process: IETEVD

**Step 1 - I** — Identify the Problem: Gather information, question the
user, identify recent changes, and check documentation.

**Step 2 - E** — Establish a Theory of Probable Cause: Question the
obvious, consider multiple approaches, and research based on the
symptoms.

**Step 3 - T** — Test the Theory: Confirm your theory and verify the
root cause. (If the theory is incorrect, loop back and re-establish a
new one).

**Step 4 - E** — Establish a Plan of Action: Plan the fix, notify
affected users, consider impacts, and implement the solution.

**Step 5 - V** — Verify Full System Functionality: Confirm the
resolution works and implement preventive measures to stop the issue
from recurring.

**Step 6 - D** — Document Findings: Record the symptoms, root cause,
steps taken, resolution, and preventive measures for the knowledge base.

### The I-E-T-E-V-D Mnemonic Matrix

**Professional**\
Variation 1: I Expect To Execute Very Decisively”\
Variation 2: “Investigate Every Technical Error Very Deliberately”

**Easy to Recall**\
Variation 1: “I Eat Tacos Every Valentine's Day”\
Variation 2: “Impatient Elephants Trample Every Village Daily”

**Quirky / Memorable**\
Variation 1: Iguanas Enjoy Teasing Earthworms Very Deliberately”\
Variation 2: “I Enjoy Taming Energetic Velociraptors Diligently”

### Web Browsing

**Memory hook:** *“80 is plain, 443 is protected” - just two numbers, no
phrase needed*

**80**\
Protocol: HTTP\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: Hypertext Transfer Protocol. Unencrypted web browsing.
Never use for sensitive data - traffic can be intercepted.

**443**\
Protocol: HTTPS\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: HTTP Secure. Encrypted web browsing using TLS/SSL. Lock
icon in browser. Standard for all modern websites.

**Quirky Memory Hooks:**

> ● **80** mph in a **plain** sedan vs. **443** layers of **armor** on a
> **Secured** bank vault.
>
> ● Alternatively: Imagine an **80-year-old** grandmother shouting her
> password out loud on the street (**HTTP - Unencrypted**), versus a
> secret agent with **443 gadgets** locking down a briefcase (**HTTPS -
> Secure**).

### Wi-Fi Standards Quick Reference

**802.11a**\
Name: —\
Max Speed: 54 Mbps\
Key Feature: 5 GHz only. Legacy.

**802.11b**\
Name: —\
Max Speed: 11 Mbps\
Key Feature: 2.4 GHz. Legacy.

**802.11g**\
Name: —\
Max Speed: 54 Mbps\
Key Feature: 2.4 GHz. Legacy.

**802.11n**\
Name: Wi-Fi 4\
Max Speed: 600 Mbps\
Key Feature: 2.4 & 5 GHz. MIMO introduced.

**802.11ac**\
Name: Wi-Fi 5\
Max Speed: 3.5 Gbps\
Key Feature: 5 GHz only. MU-MIMO.

**802.11ax**\
Name: Wi-Fi 6\
Max Speed: 9.6 Gbps\
Key Feature: 2.4/5 GHz. OFDMA. Best for dense environments.

**802.11ax (6E)**\
Name: Wi-Fi 6E\
Max Speed: 9.6 Gbps\
Key Feature: 6 GHz extension. Requires 6E-specific hardware.

**802.11be**\
Name: Wi-Fi 7\
Max Speed: 46 Gbps\
Key Feature: Multi-Link Operation (MLO). Latest standard.

### Crucial IP Addresses Quick Reference

**169.254.x.x**\
Type: APIPA\
What it Means on the Exam: The device cannot reach the DHCP server.

**127.0.0.1**\
Type: IPv4 Loopback\
What it Means on the Exam: Used to ping the local network card to test
if TCP/IP is working.

**::1**\
Type: IPv6 Loopback\
What it Means on the Exam: The IPv6 equivalent of 127.0.0.1.

**10.x.x.x**\
Type: Class A Private\
What it Means on the Exam: A non-routable private IP (used heavily in
corporate networks).

**192.168.x.x**\
Type: Class C Private\
What it Means on the Exam: A non-routable private IP (used heavily in
home/SOHO networks).

### Security Protocols Quick Reference

**WEP** — Broken - cracks in minutes. Never use.

**WPA (TKIP)** — Weak - has known vulnerabilities. Do not use.

**WPA2 (AES/CCMP)** — Current baseline standard. Use if WPA3
unavailable.

**WPA3 (SAE)** — Latest standard. Strongest protection. Use when
possible.

**SSL** — Deprecated. Replaced by TLS. Do not use.

**TLS 1.2 / 1.3** — Current standard for encrypting data in transit. TLS
1.3 is preferred.

**BitLocker** — Windows full-disk encryption. Requires TPM.
Pro/Enterprise only.

**FileVault** — macOS full-disk encryption. Built into all macOS
versions.

### Malware Mnemonic

**“Very Wild Tigers Sneak Rapidly Around Forests, Keeping trouble.”**

Each word maps to a malware type:

> ● **Very** → Virus
>
> ● **Wild** → Worm
>
> ● **Tigers** → Trojan
>
> ● **Sneak** → Spyware
>
> ● **Rapidly** → Ransomware
>
> ● **Around** → Adware
>
> ● **Forests** → Fileless Malware
>
> ● **Keeping** → Keylogger
>
> ● **Trouble** → Rootkit

### Social Engineering Mnemonic

**“People Very Smart Will Quickly Identify Tricky Pretexts.”**

Each word maps to an attack type:

> ● **People** → Phishing (general fraudulent messages)
>
> ● **Very** → Vishing (voice‑based phishing)
>
> ● **Smart** → Smishing (SMS/text phishing)
>
> ● **Will** → Whaling (executives targeted)
>
> ● **Quickly** → Quishing (QR code phishing)
>
> ● **Identify** → Impersonation (pretending to be trusted staff/vendor)
>
> ● **Tricky** → Tailgating/Piggybacking (physical access by following)
>
> ● **Pretexts** → Pretexting (fabricated scenario to gain info)

### File Transfer

**Memory hook:** *“21 to connect, 20 to send” - control channel first,
then data. SFTP rides SSH so it uses port 22.*

**Port 20 / 21**\
Protocol: FTP\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: File Transfer Protocol. Port 21 = control channel
(commands). Port 20 = data channel (file transfer). Unencrypted — avoid
on public networks.

**Port 22**\
Protocol: SFTP\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: SSH File Transfer Protocol. Secure file transfer over SSH
tunnel. Replaces FTP. Same port as SSH.

Quirky Memory Hooks:

> ● **21** keys to open the **Control** tower, **20** trucks to move the
> actual **Data.**
>
> ● Since **SFTP** relies completely on **SSH**, it steals SSH’s
> **s**eat and rides **s**hotgun on **Port 22**.

### Secure Access

**Memory hook:** *“22 is always secure, 23 is never secure” - one number
apart, opposite security*

**Port 22**\
Protocol: SSH\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: Secure Shell. Encrypted command-line remote access to
Linux/Unix servers and network devices. Replaces Telnet.

**Port 23**\
Protocol: Telnet\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: Unencrypted remote access. Completely obsolete - passwords
sent in plain text. Never use on production systems.

Quirky Memory Hooks:

> ● Port **22 SSH** is a **S**uper **S**ecure **H**aven.
>
> ● Port **23** is a **Tel**-net (**Te**ll-it-to-the-internet) with zero
> safety nets. One number apart, completely opposite worlds.

### Network Services

**Memory hook:** *“53 names things, 67/68 assigns addresses, 123 keeps
time”*

**Port 53**\
Protocol: DNS\
TCP/UDP: TCP/UDP\
Description: Domain Name System. Translates domain names (comptia.org)
to IP addresses. UDP for queries, TCP for zone transfers.

**Port 67 / 68**\
Protocol: DHCP\
TCP/UDP: UDP\
Description: Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol. Port 67 = server. Port
68 = client. Automatically assigns IP addresses. APIPA (169.254.x.x) =
DHCP failure.

**Port 123**\
Protocol: NTP\
TCP/UDP: UDP\
Description: Network Time Protocol. Synchronizes system clocks across
devices. Wrong clock = all SSL certificates appear invalid.

**Port 161 / 162**\
Protocol: SNMP\
TCP/UDP: UDP\
Description: Simple Network Management Protocol. Port 161 = queries to
devices. Port 162 = trap alerts from devices. Used for network
monitoring.

**Port 5060 / 5061**\
Protocol: SIP\
TCP/UDP: TCP/UDP\
Description: Session Initiation Protocol. Sets up and manages VoIP
calls. Port 5060 = plain. Port 5061 = encrypted TLS version.

**Quirky Memory Hooks:**

> ● Port 53 DNS: A 53 year old **D**igital **N**ame **S**pellbook
> (translates names to numbers).
>
> ● 67/68 DHCP: Think of an old-school diner where the **(67)** server
> **d**ishes to **h**ungry **c**lients.
>
> ● 123: *Tick-tock-tick-tock* (1-2-3**, clock time**!).
>
> ● Port 161/162 **SNMP**: The **S**py **N**etwork **M**anagement
> **P**rotocol. Agent **161 asks the questions**, Agent **162 sets the
> trap.**
>
> ● 5060/5061: **S**ets **I**nbound **P**hones on your **network
> lines**.

### Network Types Mnemonic

**“Please Let Wireless Make Wide Waves.”**

Each word maps to a network type:

> ● **Please** → PAN (Personal Area Network) — Bluetooth headset,
> smartwatch.
>
> ● **Let** → LAN (Local Area Network) — Home/office wired network.
>
> ● **Wireless** → WLAN (Wireless LAN) — Wi‑Fi version of LAN.
>
> ● **Make** → MAN (Metropolitan Area Network) — City‑wide, ISP‑managed.
>
> ● **Wide** → WAN (Wide Area Network) — Large geographic distances, the
> internet.
>
> ● **Waves** → WWAN (Wireless WAN) — Cellular towers, mobile broadband.

### Remote Access

**Memory hook:** *“3389 = Windows Remote Desktop, 5900 = cross-platform
VNC” - always protect both behind a VPN*

**Port 3389**\
Protocol: RDP\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: Remote Desktop Protocol. Windows graphical remote desktop.
Full desktop experience. NEVER expose directly to internet - always use
VPN.

**Port 5900**\
Protocol: VNC\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: Virtual Network Computing. Cross-platform screen sharing.
Tools: TightVNC, RealVNC. Always protect behind VPN or SSH tunnel.

**Quirky Memory Hooks:**

> ● **3389:** Three-three-eight-nine, Remote Desktop on the line!
>
> ● **5900 (VNC): V**ery **N**osey **C**olleagues have 59 ways to spy on
> your 00 (eyeballs/screen)

### Directory & File Sharing

**Memory hook:** *“389 looks up people (Active Directory), 445 shares
files (Windows), 636 does 389 securely”*

**Port 389**\
Protocol: LDAP\
TCP/UDP: TCP/UDP\
Description: Lightweight Directory Access Protocol. Queries Active
Directory for user accounts and group memberships. Unencrypted.

**Port 636**\
Protocol: LDAPS\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: LDAP over SSL. Encrypted version of LDAP. Use instead of
port 389 wherever possible.

**Port 445**\
Protocol: SMB\
TCP/UDP: TCP\
Description: Server Message Block. Windows file and printer sharing.
Common ransomware attack vector - keep patched and firewalled.

Quirky Memory Hooks:

> ● **389:** “Three-eight-nine, Active Directory is on the line!”
>
> ● **636:** “Six-three-six, secures the directory mix!”
>
> ● **445:** “Four-four-five, Windows file shares come alive!”

### Number Patterns to Memorize

**Always 22** — SSH and SFTP - if it’s secure remote access or secure
file transfer, it’s port 22

**Sequential pairs** — 20/21 = FTP (control/data) 67/68 = DHCP
(server/client)

**Plain → encrypted** — 110 → 995 (POP3) 143 → 993 (IMAP) 389 → 636
(LDAP) 80 → 443 (HTTP) 5060 → 5061 (SIP)

**Four digits** — 3389 = RDP 5060/5061 = SIP 5900 = VNC - the only
four-digit ports you need

**UDP only** — 67/68 DHCP 123 NTP 161/162 SNMP - fast, fire-and-forget
services use UDP

### Cloud Service Models

**SaaS — Software as a Service** — “You use it - they run it.” Like a
Coffee Shop - you just show up and drink . Examples: Gmail, Microsoft
365, Salesforce.

**PaaS — Platform as a Service** — “You build on it - they manage the
platform.” Like a Pottery class - they give you the wheel, you make the
pot. Examples: Azure App Service, Google App Engine

**IaaS — Infrastructure as a Service** — “You control the VMs - they own
the hardware.” Like IKEA - you get the parts, you build it yourself.
Examples: AWS EC2, Azure VMs.

### Cable Categories & Speeds Quick Reference

**Cat 5e**\
Max Speed: 1 Gbps\
Max Distance: 100 meters\
Notes: Minimum requirement for Gigabit networks.

**Cat 6**\
Max Speed: 10 Gbps\
Max Distance: 55 meters\
Notes: Drops to 1 Gbps at full 100m distance and 10 Gbps at up to 55m

**Cat 6a**\
Max Speed: 10 Gbps\
Max Distance: 100 meters\
Notes: “a” stands for augmented. Handles full distance.

**Cat 8**\
Max Speed: 40 Gbps\
Max Distance: 30 meters\
Notes: Used primarily in data centers for short server runs.

### USB & Thunderbolt Speeds Quick Reference

**USB 2.0**\
Max Speed: 480 Mbps\
Connector Types: Type-A, Type-B, Mini, Micro

**USB 3.2 Gen 1 (USB 3.0)**\
Max Speed: 5 Gbps\
Connector Types: Type-A, Type-B, Type-C

**USB 3.2 Gen 2 (USB 3.1)**\
Max Speed: 10 Gbps\
Connector Types: Type-A, Type-C

**USB 4**\
Max Speed: 40 Gbps\
Connector Types: Type-C only

**Thunderbolt 3 / 4**\
Max Speed: 40 Gbps\
Connector Types: Type-C only

### Command-Line Rosetta Stone (Windows vs. Linux)

**View IP Address**\
Windows Command: ipconfig\
Linux / macOS Command: ifconfig or ip a

**Trace Network Route**\
Windows Command: tracert\
Linux / macOS Command: traceroute

**Test Connectivity**\
Windows Command: ping\
Linux / macOS Command: ping

**View Running Processes**\
Windows Command: tasklist\
Linux / macOS Command: ps or top

**Kill a Process**\
Windows Command: taskkill\
Linux / macOS Command: kill

**Elevate Permissions**\
Windows Command: Run as Administrator\
Linux / macOS Command: sudo

### Essential Windows Run Commands Quick Reference

Launch these tools directly from the Run dialog (Win + R) or Command
Prompt

**compmgmt.msc**\
Tool It Opens: Computer Management\
What It’s Used For: The master console. Accesses Event Viewer, Task
Scheduler, and local users/groups all in one place.

**devmgmt.msc**\
Tool It Opens: Device Manager\
What It’s Used For: Used to update, roll back, or disable hardware
drivers. Look here for the dreaded “yellow exclamation mark.”

**diskmgmt.msc**\
Tool It Opens: Disk Management\
What It’s Used For: Used to initialize new hard drives, format
partitions, and assign drive letters.

**certmgr.msc**\
Tool It Opens: Certificate Manager\
What It’s Used For: Manages digital certificates for the current user or
computer, used for encryption, code signing, and authentication.

**lusrmgr.msc**\
Tool It Opens: Local Users and Groups.\
What It’s Used For: Manages local (non-domain) user accounts and group
memberships. Not available on Windows Home editions.

**gpedit.msc**\
Tool It Opens: Local Group Policy Editor.\
What It’s Used For: Configures local Group Policy settings on a single
machine (not domain-wide). Requires Windows Pro or higher — not
available on Home.

**services.msc**\
Tool It Opens: Services\
What It’s Used For: Used to start, stop, or restart background Windows
services (like the Print Spooler).

**mstsc**\
Tool It Opens: Remote Desktop Connection\
What It’s Used For: Think “Microsoft Terminal Services Client.” Opens
the RDP window to connect to another PC.

**msinfo32**\
Tool It Opens: System Information\
What It’s Used For: Displays a massive, read-only list of hardware
resources, components, and software environments.

**dxdiag**\
Tool It Opens: DirectX Diagnostic Tool\
What It’s Used For: Used to test and verify audio and video hardware
functionality.

**regedit**\
Tool It Opens: Registry Editor\
What It’s Used For: The central database for Windows settings.

> **⚠️ Exam Trap:** Never edit the registry without backing it up first!
>
> **✔ Pro Tip:** If a command manages a specific system component
> through a graphical console, it almost always ends in .msc (Microsoft
> Management Console). If it opens a standalone tool, it usually just
> requires the tool’s name (like cmd, taskmgr, or mstsc)

### Essential Linux Commands Quick Reference

**ls**\
Tool/Action: List files\
What It’s Used For: View directory contents. Equivalent to dir in
Windows.

**pwd**\
Tool/Action: Print working directory\
What It’s Used For: Shows current directory path.

**cd**\
Tool/Action: Change directory\
What It’s Used For: Navigate between folders.

**sudo**\
Tool/Action: Superuser do\
What It’s Used For: Run commands with elevated privileges. Equivalent to
“Run as Administrator.”

**chmod**\
Tool/Action: Change mode\
What It’s Used For: Modify file/folder permissions.

**chown**\
Tool/Action: Change ownership\
What It’s Used For: Assign ownership of files/folders.

**ps / top**\
Tool/Action: Process status\
What It’s Used For: View running processes. Equivalent to tasklist.

**kill**\
Tool/Action: Kill process\
What It’s Used For: Terminate a process. Equivalent to taskkill.

**ifconfig / ip a**\
Tool/Action: Network config\
What It’s Used For: View IP address and network settings. Equivalent to
ipconfig.

**traceroute**\
Tool/Action: Trace route\
What It’s Used For: Diagnose network path issues. Equivalent to tracert.

**apt-get / yum / dnf**\
Tool/Action: Package managers\
What It’s Used For: Install/remove software packages. Equivalent to
Windows Installer.

**systemd**\
Tool/Action: Init system\
What It’s Used For: Modern Linux service manager — boots the system,
starts services, manages system state.

**/etc/resolv.conf\**
Tool/Action: Config file**\**
What It’s Used For: Specifies which DNS servers the system uses for name
resolution.

**/etc/fstab\**
Tool/Action: Config file**\**
What It’s Used For: Lists devices/partitions to auto-mount at boot, with
mount points and options.

### Essential macOS Commands & Tools (Quick Reference)

**Finder** — File manager for navigation/storage.

**System Settings** — Configure network, users, displays, privacy

**Terminal** — Command-Line Interface (CLI) for advanced tasks.

**diskutil** — Partition/format drives (CLI).

**open** — Launch apps/files from Terminal.

**Activity Monitor** — CPU, memory, disk, network usage.

**Time Machine** — Built-in backup with snapshots

**Mission Control** — Overview of all open windows, desktops (Spaces),
and full-screen apps for quick navigation.

**Rapid Security Response (RSR)** — Apple's mechanism for delivering
critical patches outside the normal full OS update cycle

### Backup Types: Full, Incremental, Differential

> ● Full = Everything. Slowest to create, fastest to restore.
>
> ● Incremental = Changes since LAST backup. Fastest to create, slowest
> to restore (need all incrementals).
>
> ● Differential = Changes since LAST FULL. Medium both ways. Need last
> full + latest diff only.

## Exam Traps & Wrong Answers

These are the most common trick questions and misconceptions on the A+
exam. Review these carefully before test day.

### Hardware Traps

**Degaussing an SSD to erase data**

— WRONG - degaussing only works on magnetic media. SSDs use flash
memory. Use Secure Erase or physical destruction for SSDs.

**Formatting a drive before disposal makes data unrecoverable**

— WRONG - format only removes the file system metadata. Data can be
recovered. Use proper data wiping or physical destruction.

**DDR4 RAM works in a DDR5 slot**

— WRONG - DDR4 and DDR5 have different notch positions. They are
physically incompatible.

**Dual-channel requires any two sticks in any two slots**

— WRONG - DIMMs must be matched pairs in the CORRECT slot pair (e.g.,
slots 1+3 or 2+4). Check the motherboard manual.

**Replacing a laptop CPU requires removing the motherboard first**

— Only true if the CPU is NOT soldered. Modern laptops often have
soldered CPUs that cannot be replaced at all.

### Networking Traps

**IMEI is used for roaming charges**

— WRONG - IMSI identifies the subscriber for billing/roaming. IMEI
identifies the hardware device.

**An APIPA address means the network is working**

— WRONG - APIPA (169.254.x.x) means the device FAILED to get a DHCP
address. It’s a fallback, not a success.

**Fiber optic cables are affected by EMI**

— WRONG - fiber uses light, not electrical signals. It is immune to
electromagnetic interference. That’s one of its key advantages.

**A switch broadcasts to all ports like a hub**

— WRONG - a switch sends frames only to the correct destination port
using MAC address tables. A HUB broadcasts to all ports.

**DNS assigns IP addresses automatically**

— WRONG - DHCP assigns IP addresses. DNS translates domain names to IP
addresses.

### OS & Security Traps

**Remote Desktop works the same on Windows Home and Pro**

— WRONG - Windows Home can be a Remote Desktop CLIENT (connect to
others) but cannot be a HOST (be connected to). Pro/Enterprise required
to host.

**BitLocker is available on Windows Home**

— WRONG - BitLocker requires Windows Pro, Pro for Workstations, or
Enterprise. Windows Home does not include BitLocker.

**Deleting files and emptying the Recycle Bin permanently removes data**

— WRONG - it only removes the file pointer. Data remains until
overwritten. Use proper wiping tools for secure deletion.

**Private/Incognito mode makes browsing anonymous**

— WRONG - it only prevents LOCAL history, cookies, and cache from being
saved. ISPs, employers, and websites can still see all traffic.

**Antivirus software alone is sufficient security**

— WRONG - defense in depth requires multiple layers: AV + firewall +
patch management + user education + MFA + encryption.

### Mobile & Cloud Traps

**Remote Wipe should be used first when a phone is missing**

— WRONG - use Remote LOCK first. Remote Wipe permanently erases
everything. Lock first; wipe only if confirmed stolen.

**PRL updates must be done manually by the user**

— WRONG - PRL updates are pushed automatically via OTA (Over-The-Air)
updates from the carrier.

**SaaS means the customer manages the underlying infrastructure**

— WRONG - with SaaS, the provider manages everything including the
application, platform, and infrastructure. The customer just uses it.

**Public AI tools are safe for entering customer data**

— WRONG - data entered into public AI tools may be used for training and
is not guaranteed to be private. Never enter PII or sensitive data.

## The 8-Week “Zero-to-Certified” Sprint

**Week 1: Mobile Devices & Laptops**

> ● Master smartphone/tablet features, laptop hardware components, input
> devices, and mobile connectivity options.

**Week 2: Networking Fundamentals**

> ● Learn network types, TCP/IP, IP addressing, ports & protocols,
> DNS/DHCP, VLANs, and SOHO network setup.

**Week 3: Hardware & Peripherals**

> ● Focus on motherboards, CPUs, RAM, storage (SSD/HDD/RAID), printers,
> expansion cards, and power supplies.

**Week 4: Virtualization, Cloud & IoT**

> ● Understand hypervisors, VM configuration, cloud models
> (IaaS/PaaS/SaaS), and IoT device security.

**Week 5: Operating Systems**

> ● Master Windows tools (CMD/PowerShell/Task Manager), Linux commands,
> macOS basics, and OS installation/upgrade paths.

**Week 6: Security**

> ● Learn threat types, authentication methods, wireless security
> (WPA3/SAE), malware removal, and physical security controls

**Week 7: Troubleshooting & Operations**

> ● Practice hardware/software/network troubleshooting methodology,
> professional communication, and IT documentation.

**Week 8: Review & Practice Exams**

> ● Take full practice exams for both Core 1 (220-1201) and Core 2
> (220-1202), review weak areas, and schedule your exams..

# Practice Test

This practice test covers all modules from both Core 1 and Core 2. Try
to answer each question before looking at the answers. Time yourself -
aim to answer each question in under 60 seconds.

### Part 1: Hardware (Core 1)

> ● **A technician is replacing RAM in a desktop. The motherboard has 4
> DIMM slots labeled A1, A2, B1, B2. To enable dual-channel mode, which
> slots should be populated first?**

1.  A1 and A2

2.  A1 and B1

3.  A2 and B2

4.  Any two adjacent slots

> ● **Which connector provides the main power from the PSU to a modern
> desktop motherboard?**

1.  8-pin EPS

2.  4-pin Molex

3.  24-pin ATX

4.  6+2 pin PCIe

> ● **A technician replaces an aging HDD with an NVMe SSD. After
> installation, the system does not detect the new drive. What is the
> MOST likely cause?**

1.  The SATA cable is disconnected

2.  The BIOS/UEFI needs to be updated to support NVMe

3.  The NVMe drive is defective

4.  NVMe drives require a PCIe power cable

> ● **Which RAID level requires a minimum of 4 drives and can survive
> two simultaneous drive failures?**

1.  RAID 0

2.  RAID 5

3.  RAID 6

4.  RAID 10

> ● **A laser printer is producing pages where toner smears when
> touched. Which component is MOST likely failing?**

1.  Imaging drum

2.  Charging roller

3.  Fuser assembly

4.  Transfer belt

> ● **A user reports their laptop screen is very dim, but connecting an
> external monitor shows a clear image. What component is MOST likely
> faulty?**

1.  GPU

2.  Digitizer

3.  Inverter

4.  LVDS cable

> ● **Degaussing is an effective data destruction method for which of
> the following?**

1.  SSD

2.  USB flash drive

3.  Magnetic tape

4.  Optical disc (DVD)

> ● **A laptop connects to Wi-Fi but receives a 169.254.x.x IP address.
> What does this indicate?**

1.  The laptop is connected to a 5 GHz network

2.  DHCP server is unreachable

3.  The DNS server is down

4.  WPA3 authentication failed

    240W power supplies that support charging via USB-C. The IT
